PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

Mats is a sign-post: he can point out the right way for others, but he can't go himself. MIKE: "Sure, toimes is moighty har-r-ud. There's bin no wur-r-uk at the mill for a mont.." Pat: "Why don't yez go on shtrike ? A GREAT deal has been said as to the slowness of the turtle's movements, but a!1 we can say is, he generally arrives in time for soup. THE JUDGE: " What is your age, madam ?" Fair Respondent : "Your lordship, I leave that to the mercy of the Court." There are occasions when a man feels that a real good sneeze would do him more good than a big legacy. JUDGE GUFFEY : " What passed between yourself and the complainant?" O'Brien: "I think, sor, half a dozen bricks and a piece of pavin'-stone." WILLY'S RUSE—Willy: I heard papa say that sugar is very fiAtening, mamma." Mamma: "That is true, Willy." " Willy: "Then, mamma, won t you give me a couple of lumps for the greyhound ? " His SUSPICIONS WERE CONFIRMED.—A. : "I've forgotten more than you ever knew." B. "I thought you'd met with some such misfortune as that. GOOD GROUNDS FOR PROPHECY.—Van Sharp: "I hear Gussie Hardluck is very ill. How is he getting on, doctor ? " Dr. Pillbox: "I don't think he will live a month." Van Sharp: "Who is attending him ? ' Dr. Pillbox (with some hesita- tion): "1 am." WHERE THE COST COMES IN.—Friend : "Is yachting an expensive amusement ?" Miss De • Style: "Awfully !" "I should not think those little sail-boats would cost very much." "Oh, the boats are cheap enough I It's the clothes." LITTLE JOHNNY KNEW.—Sunday School teacher: "Children, do you know the house that is open to all—to the poor, the rich, and sad and happy ;to man and woman ;to the old and young? Do you know the house I mean?" Little Johnny: " Yeth, ma'am, I know." Sunday ' School Teacher: Well, Johnny, what house is it ? " Little Johnny: "The police-station. SILENCE is only golden when you cannot think of a good answer. Every singer in a quartet can give three good reasons why the organisation isn't absolutely perfect. EXTRACT FROM A CELEBRATED DENTIST'S Aknum.—" It has been stated that man in a savage state never suffers tooth ache. lam rather inclined to think that a man with a tooth-ache is in a savage state." A SIX-YEAR OLD little fellow was forced to wear a shirt three sizes too large for him. After strutting around a little while, he burst out with: "Ma, I feel awful lonesome in this shirt 1" EASY FOR Ilim.—Superientendent : "Do you think you could fill the position of timekeeper in our boiler factory? The noise is very trying for one not used to it." Applicant (contemptuously): " Hump ! I used to be the janitor of a public school." A GIRL forced by her parents into a disagreeable match with an old man, whom she detested, when the clergyman came to that part of the service where the bride is asked if she consents to take the bridegroom for her husband, said, with great simplicity :—" Oh, dear, no, sir; but you are the first person who has asked my opinion about the matter." A LECTURER was dilating upon the strength of the magnet, defying anyone to show or name any- thing surpassing its powers. A hearer demurred, and instanced a young lady, who used to attract him thirteen miles every Sunday. BIS SISTER'S Smolt : "Here, Ralph, is a six- pence; now tell me what your sister says about me." Ralph: "Gimme another one, and I won't tell you what she says about you." VERY UNPOPULAR.—" I wonder what makes Higby so unpopular?" "I give it up, but it is a fact. Why, that man is so disliked that he can't even get a bite when he goes fishing." HE: "It was rather strange that you should have clear weather throughout the voyage." She: "Not at all. I was told that the captain swept the Nky with his telescope the first thing every morning." JOBMASTER "I'm afraid, sir, I must ask you to pay in .dvance for the hire of the horse." Amateur Rider : "What's that or? Are you afraid that I shall come back withoft the horse? " Jobmaster "Oh, no, sir I But the horse might come back without you!" LADY (on leaving a crowded tramcar) : " Well, I'm glad to get out of this crush. I've had to stand on one foot all the way." Voice from Ccrner "Yes, and that one foot was mine." DORA : "Papa said we musn't encourage tramps, because one tells all the rest." Clara : " I couldn't help it, he looked so starved." " What did you give him?" "Half a cream puff and some caramels." COMING TO THE POINT.—" Papa, what is a conflagration ? ' "A great fire, my boy." "And what name would you give to a small fire, then?" "A small fire? I cannot think just now of any special word. You might call it an incipient fire, or a fire of little moment." "Well, pap.; I have just noticed that your coat was burning at the back, and I was wondering whether it was a conflagration or an incipient fire I" "THERE'S one fine thing about our wedding pre- sents, Tom, dear," she said. "There wasn t a duplicate in the lot." "That's true; worse luck," sighed Tom. "We haven't any excuse for -disposing of any of 'em now, and times are awfully hard." MRS. TEA: "And how is your neighbour?" Mrs. Bee: She's well enough, I suppose. I havn't seen her to speak to for six weeks." Mrs. Tea: "Why, I thought you were on the most friendly terms." Mrs. Bee: "Well, we used to be; but we've exchanged servants." CORONER: "You seem to be certain that the deceased accidently fell into the water. How do you know that this is not a case of suicide?" Witness: "He was a brither, Scot, and had a wee bottle o' whisky on him wi' ne'er a nip ta'en oot." SHE : "Dear me, Walter, these are terrible things you tell me about Arthur. How do you happen to know, so much of him?" He (a rival of Arthur's for her hand) : "Why, Daisy, I'm his best friend." Miss THIRTY-EIGHT (coyly) : "What a pity it seems, Mr. Somerset, that a man like you should be a bachelor." Mr. Somerset : " Miss aliirty-eight, it does seem a pity, but I can't help it, you know. I was born so." ETHEL : "IS Clara so very much in love?" Maud: "I should say she is. I saw her writing a letter to him the other day, and she put Dear "ack ' on the enevelone."

===== 0002601_18950309_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
" CAPITA]," CHAT.

--- The London Radicals are quite unable to minim- ise their defeat of Saturday, and, to their credit, they are not attempting to do so. Some of their leaders have already dubbed the day "Black Satur- day." Their enemies speak of the defeat in the picturesque and vigorous phrase as "the rout of the Radicals;" and even sober-minded Liberals ask "What do we keep?" A number of strong and well-known London reformers has gone down in the fray, and their loss will considerably weaken the hands, and increase the responsibilities, of those who remain. But the most prominent men of the Council have come back to it—the men who shaped I its policy, presented its work to the public, and in- spired their followers with ideas. In other words. the great administrators of the Council have returned to it. The chairman and vice-chairman, Mr. Sidney Webb, Mr. Costelloe, Mr. Benn, Mr. John Burns, and some other leading spirits, are all there again, shorn, of course of their old influence, since the majority at their back is gone. Mr. Sidney Webb attributes the Radical defeat to the desertion of the middle classes; the other Radicals ascribe it to the apathy of the working men. It is by the loss of seats in the South and the East of London that the Progressive majority has been des- troyed; and Woolwich, Rotherhithe, and the like are eminently industrial in their character. Even in Radical Battersea, Mr. John Burns's vote has declined by 1,200, and instead of retaining the first he now takes the second place.

One has heard so much of the Independent Labour party of late, and what it boasts it can do and intends to do, at the general parliamentary election, that its operations during the present campaign became an object worthy ot examination. The party put nine candidates into the field, and the whole nine polled a very few over a thousand votes. They did not carry a single seat, but their intrusion lost four seats to the Progressives. They are absolutely powerless as a self-dependent politi- cal force, and only become formidable as a mere spoiler of the chances of Radical candidates. But their inflvence over their adherents must not be taken at a low estimate, as their advice respecting abstention may be the explanation of at least some part or the decline in the Progressive vote. At the moment I am writing the exact diminution of the Progressive vote is not known to me ; but, by the results already announced, 27,000 fewer Progres- sives went to the poll than went in 1892. Mr. Keir Hardie will no doubt claim a large proportion of these. In the old-fashioned classifieation. they are reckoned simply as Progressives. " Yes," Mr.Hardie will say," Progressives, with a difference." But even if he could claim credit for the whole decline of 27,000, what does Labuur gain by their absten- tion? Ile has let in the ground landlord, the re- actionary who ridicules the payment of trades union wages, the Tory who scoffs at the municipa- lising of tramways, gasworks, and water supply, and the readjustment of taxation.

For nearly forty years, Professor Blackie, poet, philosopher, patriot, and professor of Greek at Edinburgh,who died on Sat urday,has figured as one of the most interesting,certainly the mostpictures- que, of our public men. He used to call himself a " modern Sophist," in the original sense of that term ; and there were few subjects—in the range,at least, of history, poetry, and philosophy—on which he did not deem himself competent to hold a pub- lic disputation. His ideas and his eccentricities were the talk of the University of which be was so picturesque an ornament. He was always an ardent advocate, and indeed practitioner of Scottish song and he has left behind him several volumesof verse which his countrymen will not readily let die. In an age of pessimism he held high the banner of human hope and human aims; and he was never accused of an unworthy action.

The fundamental proposal in Mr. Bryce's Bill for the settlement cf trade disputes is not finding ac- ceptance even on his own side of the House, and I believe the measure will come out of the Grand Committee very different from vthat it is now. Opinion runs in two directions at present, one sec- tion of members being in favour of giving the local authorities of such great cities as Liverpool and Manchester full power to appoint boards of con- ciliation, untrammelled by the Board of Trade, and another section being in favour of a high and in- fluential tribunal being appointed for the whole nation, consisting, say, of a certain number of judges, who, on a trade dispute being brought be- fore them, should have power to investigate the matters in dispute, even by examining the em- ployer's books. In neither case has it been argued ' that the award of the tribunal should be comi,ul- - and the question therefore restricts itself al- most to this—whether the greatest impartiality and weight might be expected in a local tribunal or in a national one. A 'second point may also arise, namely, whether, if a Government depart- ment is to have any function in the matter at all, it should not rather be the Home Office than the Board of Trade. The latter may have itspartisans ; the Home Office has certainly found a champion in Sir John Gorst. Bverything depends on the man at the head of the department. If Mr. Asquith, for example, were to go to the Board of Trade to- morrow, it is impossible to believe that he would not make his powerful personality felt there quite as much as he has done at the Home Office.

-- Mr. Clement Higgins, Q.C., M.P. for Mid-Norfolk, is no longer in a state of suspended political devel- opment, as he had dissociated himself entirely from his old Liberal friends and joined the Liberal Unionists. He has requested that the circulars of the Liberal Unionist party may in future be sent to ' him • and as so very small a political party cannot afford to raise any difficulties as to the soundness of a convert's views, he has been graciously admitted to the ranks of the faithful few who follow Mr. , Chamberlain. But he ought to submit himself once i more to his constituents, as his former appearance on the stool of political repentance had reference merely to the action of the Government in connec- tion with the House of Lords. He now appears as • a complete reactionary, and his constituents must he devoid of spirit if they do not now call upon him to resign his seat.

-- It appears to be generally understood among the members of the Unemployed Committee that the interim report which will be submitted to Parlia- ment will make no special recommendations, but will merely state that the evidence taken does not permit them at the present stage to urge that any exceptional steps should be taken. The evidence given up to the present does not appear to justify the view taken by Mr. Keir Hardie and his friends that exceptional distress prevails.

It is now stated that the report of the Royal Commission on the Aged Poor has received the signatures of most of the members. Some who are not satisfied with the findings of the majority have signed a minority report framed by Mr. James Stuart, which makes some practical recommends- I tions in the direction of the extension of outdoor relief to aged persons, instead of compelling them first to enter the workhouse. There will be other minority reports or schemes, signed respectively by Mr. Chamberlain and Mr. Broadhurst, and the report and appendices are expected to be ready for issue to the public in about ten days' time.

Mr. Florence O'Driscoll, the Irish member who has just saved his life by swimming a mile and a half at Perth, West Australia. is very well-known among members of the House, having acted for the last two sessions as Whip of the National party. Air. O'Driscoll is a tall, well-built, muscular Irish- man, who has spent the greater portion of his life in the Australian bush. Curiously enough, this is the second occasion on which he has saved himself by his abilities as a swimmer.

It is doubtful if Sir Joseph! Weston's death will lead to an election contest at Bristol. The seat is so thoroughly safe for the Radicals that, the Union- ists would be badly advised to expose themselves to a crushing disaster. When Sir Joseph came for- ward in 1890 he was returned with a solid majority ot 2,875, and that, too, though there was a Labour candidate in the field to split the Liberal vote. In 1892 no contest took place, in consequence of a friendly arrangement between Sir Joseph on the one side, and Sir M. F. Reach on the other, for an unopposed return. But, going back to the Home Rule split, the Liberal majority was still nearly 2,000; and in the face of these facts, and on the eve of a general election, the Liberal candidate is not likelyito encounter opposition. •

• The returns of the election for the London County Council were completed on Monday by the declarattoi s in the five divisions left over from Saturday. The elec- tion has resulted in a tie, each party having returned 59 members. The Prmoisives lave lost 24 seats, and gained one.

LADIES' LETTER.

UT DEAREST NELLIE,

You must be glad the frost has gone. lam sure I am, for we can begin to think about spring and spring dresses. Even if we do get a return of winter there is no harm in preparing for the spring which must come before long. The Dowager Duchess of Sutherland is the" director" of a mining company. In all New York the richest lady is not yet six years old. Her mother, Mrs. Terry, is worth four millions sterling, but the little one is a millionaire ten times over in her own right. Mrs. Yates, of Springfield, Ohio, is quite a champion in her own way. She is the mother of twenty-tour children. Among them are five sets of twins. She was married at the age of fourteen, and her eldest son is not yet thirty. When the Empress of Austria announces her intention of going for a walk her maids of honour tremble. She is an untiring pedestrian, and frequently walks twenty miles at a stretch, and this at a pace equal to that of a man's. Consequently there are very few attendants who are able to keep up with her. Some of them are permitted to accompany their Royal mistress on horse- back. Mrs. Fenwick Miller, in speaking the other day, says that at present only eighteen per cent. of English girls are married, and only twenty-five per cent. of American There are t3O obvious reasons, Mrs. Miller says, for this fact—to wit, the first, that men do not care to marry educated girls; second, that cultivated women who cars earn their own living and have their own careers do not care to marry the men they see around. Mrs. Fenwick Miller thinks that if men could see how often they were accepted because gills see no other chances of a liveli- hood in the future, they might not be pleased ! What is a Cold? Dr. Charles E. Page classes ordinary " colds " among "filth diseases." But by this he does not mean that colds are the result of uncleanliness, but of internal con- ditions. The system is surcharged with effete matter, the product of ovt r teding, over clothing, over housing, and under exercising. " Colds' " he argues, "are n.tt the result of exposure to cold and wet, but are generally the result of repletion. Coming out of the club-room on a rather sultry night, myself dressed in a light suit, and no underfiannels, and feeling delightfully comfortable, a companion, dressed in heavy suit and heavy flannels, puts on his top coat and shortly remarks that It is pretty warm.' 'Yes,' I reply, rather too warm for an overcoat.' 'That's so,' he says, ' but I don't dare leave it off.' He is utterly fearless of the smoke-laden at mos- phere of the billiard-room, where he will stay for hours at a stretch, and kicks if anyone wants a window open to dilute the poisonous air with a little stream of t resh ; and if his nose begins to ' stop up' (really from heat), he will attribute his discomfiture to not getting that window shut seen enough."

WE PARTED AT THE ALTAR;

By LAURA JEAN LIBBEY, Author of "A Mad Betrothal," "lone," "Parted by Fate," "Floribers Lover," etc., etc.

CHAPTER VII. DRIVEN OUT INTO THE VRUCTAX When Doris opened her eyes she found herself lying upon her white couch and the kindly face of the old housekeeper bending over her. "Where am I? What is the matter?" she exclaimed, struggling up to a sitting posture and pushing back the heavy mass of golden curls from her face with her little white hand; but in a flash memory returned to her, and she remembered all; and a bitter cry echoed through the room as she fell back on her pillow, turning her face to the wall. "My poor child, you must not take on like this," said the housekeeper, pityingly. "You will force yourself into brain fever.' "I wish I could!" cried Doris, bitterly. "Would to Heaven I could die and end it all. Oh, yes, yes, I wish I had fallen dead in my wedding clothes at his feet—at the altar." "My poor child," said the housekeeper, sooth- ingly, that is a horrible prayer to fall from the lips of one so young. Be brave and strong; look your trouble in the face calmly, and pray God for strength to bear it. You are not the first young wife who has been cruelly abandoned by the man she loved and trusted—the man who, at the altar, vowed to love, cherish and protect her while life lasted." "I loved him so ! " moaned Doris. "Oh, Heaven help me to bear it, or I must die. Where shall 1 turn, where shall I go ? " "I would advise you to go back to your mother," said the housekeeper. "She could comfort you best." "I have no mother," sobbed Doris, "I am alone in the great, cruel world." "You must have friends," said the house- keeper. Doris shook her golden head. "Not one on earth," she sobbed. The good housekeeper looked at the fair young girl in wonder. No friend !—not one on the wide earth ! It was almost incredible. "Are you sure the marriage you believe in so fully was a legal one ?' asked Mrs. Lane, in per- plexity. "A man who deserts a young girl delibero.tely in this fashion is capable of any vil- lainy." Doris raised her great, childish blue eyes to her

remain under this roof till you make a clean breast ; of it." The beautiful golden head was bent low before her. Doris turned her great blue eyes piteously to honest John Delmar, who stood by, regarding the scene in dazed, dumbfounded helplessness. "Forgive me, oh, won't you, please, and take me back I " sobbed Doris, distractedly, holding out her hands toward him. "v on have been kind to me all my life. Don't desert me now, for I have no one but you to look to. If you turn from me I shall surely die." "It would be the best thing you could do," returned madame, curtly. "You may as well go back where you have been for the past two days if you persist in refusing to confide in me." "Do be merciful and forgive me," cried the poor, tortured child. "I have nowhere to go, madame, God has shut me entirely out from His mercy, and forgotten me too I" " Well, that's just what I intend to do," cried madame, heartlessly and mercilessly, fiercely grasping Doris by the shoulder, and forcing her to her feet. "I want you to leave this house the same way you came. Go where you've been the past two 4ays," she repeated, tauntingly; "and if you have one spa-k of decency or pride left, you will keep out of th ; neighbours sight, or there will be worse stories afloat than there are now. For the last time I say, clear this mystery up straight- . way, or go." "Oh, ho* can I tell her? How dare I tell her," thought Doris, wildly, "that I am a victim to love's cruel curse—that he whom I wedded and trusted so blindly, deceived me so cruelly; cast me adrift on the great ocean of life, without the least pity ! "Surely, you don't mean to drive me from the shelter of your roof out into the bitter storm, madame ?" gasped Doris. "Surely, you cannot mean it," she cried, a death-like chill creeping over her. "I do mean it !" cried madame, sharply. "Go —without delay !" "Now, Cynthy, sister, don't be too hard upon poor little Doris, interposed John Delmar, in an unsteady voice. " You ve gone tar enough. You must remember, Cynthy, little Doris is only a thoughtless child. I do not believe she's done anything wrong. Have you, child ?" he asked, with intense earnestness. Doris tried to answer, but the words died away on her white lips, making no sound; and she gazed at him with a world. of pitiful entreaty in her blue

eyes. "There !" cried m,dame, shrilly and triumph- antly. "Don't you see she dare not deny it? I say again' you waste your pity." John Delmar, poor DJris's only friend, turned away, sick at heart, an i abruptly left the room, to hide the great tears that were welling down his honest face.

face. "I am sure it was legal," she answered. "-I remember he was very particular that it should be so. Oh, how can I think he has wilfully, deliber- ately deserted me ! Some day he will return here for me. I feel sure of it ; I feel it in my heart. I cannot live without him, any more than the flowers could live without the sunshine. Oh, he mv,st return to me !" How the next day passed Doris could never re- member, It seemed as though the torture of a lifetime was crowdd into it. Then it suddenly dawned upon her she must leave the hotel. . She could not remain there for ever. There was but one place she knew of to go to, and that was —back to Madame Delmar's seminary. Ah, what a desolate ride back it was to Beech Grove! The train whirled past beautiful stretches of country, tura ty villages and peaceful homes, but Dors looked out of the windows without see- ing. It was dusk when the train stopped at the station. For an hour or more a terrible storm had been raging, but Doris stepped from the train and made her way up the steep path, all unmindful of the bitter storm. Her wedding-ring fell from her fingee in the long grass by the wayside, but she did not heed its loss. Stealing in through the 'great arched gate-way, she made her way around to the western wing of the building. This had been set apart for Madame Delmar's use during vacation. The crimson hangings were looped back, and a bright flood of light shone out through the darkness like a beacon light. Noiselessly Doris approached the window nod peered into the room. Ah, what a contrast to the cheerfulness within was the driv- ing, pitiless *storm without. Drawing the rose vines aside, Doris pressed her white face still closer to the pane. At the tab c, engaged on some trifling fancy work, sat Madame Delmar, and near her stood her brother John, a plain, sturdy farmer, whom madame was always particular to ignore in public. John's farm adjoined the seminary grounds on the loft, and the awkward bachelor brother made his home in the western wing of the seminary ; bat few of madaine's dainty, aristocratic pupils ever knew this, or even dreamed of the existence of such a person. On this particular evening madame and her brother Jilin were discussing Doris's sudden dis- appearance of a few days before. Ah, how plainly every word was heard by the little figure crouch- ing out in the pitiful fs io r m ! "I always thought no good would come of Doris roaming round like a wild . gipsy for hours at a time," declared madame, stitching vigorously at her embroidery. "And now she has run away at last. I have always said no good ever comes of taking in waifs left on one's doorstep. She always wanted to .lay the line lady. Doris Brandon was too roin,mtic. l'oo much love-dreaming and non- sense go with these pretty faces." "I can't think where the child could possibly have gone, Cynthy," interposed honest John, dejectedly. "I always thought we were doing i.ur clear duty by little Doris in providing her such a good home. I never dreamed she was unhappy here. I miss the child so !" "Don't make an old fool of yourself, John Delmar !" exclaimed his sister, energetically. (She never wasted any of the little elegaincies of speech or grammar upon plain, honest John.) "It was that miserable girl's own fault if she was not happy here. She was always too fond of decking herself out in finery, to look nicer than my pupils, and they rich men's daughters, ever one of 'em. And as to missing her, the Lord knows I am glad to be rid of her I" she added with spiteful emphasis. A twig snapped beneath the windaw, and—was it only their fancy ?—a white tace, framed in tangled, golden, wet hair, peered wistfully into the room from among the climbing red roses. A moment later a shadow crept across the thres- hold, and a voice, faltering piteously, sobbed out brokenly: "Oh, madame! I have come tack to you? Oh, pity and forgive ! If you scold me I shall fall down and die at your feet l" "It's Doris's spirit !" gasped madame, in terror. " Look at that tangled hair and white, horrible face ! ' "I am no spirit, madame 1" sobbed Doris, ad- vancing toward the centre of the room, near where the horror-stricken woman stood. "I'm your unhappy ward, Doris!" "So you are really Doris in the flesh / " retorted the irate madame, her rising anger tAing the place of her momentary fright. "Where have you been for the last two days? I want to know where you have been, I say. Your dress is bedraggled with mud. We would be disgraced for life if anyone hereabouts saw you. I ask yeu again, Doris Bran- don, what's the meaning of all this?" " I—l cannot tell you, madame!" sighed Doris, bitterly. "Only be kind to me and forget that I ever went away." "Do you hear that miserable girl? Do you hear her, John?" cried madame, furiously. "This is the creature you would waste your pity on! She slips off in the night, Heaven only knows where, and I dragging the bay and scouring the whole country for her; and now, after two days, she comes coolly back, and asks me to forget the disgrace she has brought on us, and, most of all, insults us by telling us not to ask where she has been. I never struck the girl in my life yet, but I declare I can scarcely restrain myself from beating the truth out of her. I shall investigate this matter thoroughly; depend upon that, un- grateful girl." "You may kill me, if you choose, madame," sighed poor Doris, with a gasping, tearleas sob, as she sank down on the floor in a little, dark heap, "but I will not—oh, I cannot reveal to you the dark secret of the past two days. ' "Is there a lover at the bottom of it ? " cried madame, shrilly, fairly shrieking the words. "Answer me Has love anything to do with this disgraceful affair? ' "No. No one loves me," muttered Doris, faintly. "Oh, no, no ; God never intended anyone to love me I" she added, wearily. "You cannot deceive me. CHAPTER VIII. OUT IN THE DARKNESS. Wrrn a bitter cry, Doris sank on her knees at madame's feet, her beautiful, tangled, golden hair falling in reckless abandon about her like a golden veil, her face white with fear. Poor child I She might have known better by past experience than to appeal to John Delmar, when his s titer "laid down the law," as she called

the ways of city life, child. Mrs. Granville would be kind to you. "It does not matter much where I go; one place Is the same as another to me," answered Doris dr. arily. How little John Delmar thought, as he went back to the house aud penned that fatal letter, that that one incident was the turtling point of Doris's eventful life. He wrote it with a smile on his lips, little dreaming of the terrible consequences that would accrue from, it. He took her down to the railway-station, and purchased her ticket. The train was just starting, and with a hasty "Good-bye," Doris drifted out of his life forever, and on to her fate. "Have I done right in sending the child to New York?" lie pondei ed, as lie stood on the platform of the station in the down-pouring rain and watched the train out of sight, as the bright 4ht disappeared in the impenetrable darkness. "Die! York is a great, cruel, wicked city. I almost wish I had sent Doris to some quiet little village here- abouts. Somehow 1 have a vague, troubled sensa- tion about it. I should have taken time to consider the matter well. Ido not know that I have ever done anything like this on the impulse of the Ino- ment before. I hope Doris will write as soon as she reaches there.'' Carefudy placing John Delinar's letter of intro- duction in her pocket, Doris leaned back in her seat, giving herself up to her own thoughts. Wedded and cruedy deserted in the space of one short week, was ever a girl's fate more pitiful? And yet, for all this, Difis could not quite hate the handsome young husband she had met and married so romantically. The sun was high in the heavens when the train steamed into the Cl:and Central station. The con- fusion, the stir, the tusk of the great throng ef people confused Doris wonderfully. "It is worse than Baltimore,' she thought, alarm. "Cab! carriage ! hack!" cried a dozen voiceS, as many cabmen closed in around the timid, shrink ing little figure, and Doris was almost thrust int° the nearest vehicle. A short ride brought her to her destination, 10' West 32nd street. Timidly ascending the stepsj Doris rang the bell, and a servant soon appear° in answer to the summons. "If you please, I should like to see Mrs. Gran: ville. I bring a letter from Mr. John Delmar, o' Beech Grove. Tell her—" "Mrs. Granville !" repeated the man. " W,1123"; that's the name of the family that used to here. They moved away—left the city over a Year ago." "Gone !" echoed Doris, turning tale. "Gone !did you say?" "Yes,' replied the man, impatiently, " thaCs what I said, Miss." A deadly faintness seized Doris; she stood in the marble vestibule dazed, bewildered by this, terrible stroke of fate. Where was she to gai What was she to do? She turned and walke" down the steps clutching her hands tightly over her heart. Alone, friendless, and with less than a dollar in her puree, in the streets of New York ! Ah, what should she do ? That was the piteous prayer that fell from her lips, as she Toyed on down the street. " New York !” she murmured, with a suddea start. "It is here his parents live. How strange it is that I could have forgotten that, even for onie momen.." Should she go to his mother—the proud, hauglitY lady he had described, and tell he all? Oi, no, no—a thousand times No ! would rejoice that her son had left her; she woa" firri no pity, no mercy there. She had scarcely proceeded a dozen rods ere she saw a phaeton, drawn by two mettlesome ponies: rapidly approaching her. Without another word, Doris turned and fled out into the darkness. In the distance she could hear the murmur of the waves as they beat drearily upon the shore. "I wish I had flung myself into the water when I intended to, that night, when those fatal words bound me to him—his wedded wife. "His wife ! Oh, dear Heaven !" she cried bit- terly, struggling on through the terrible storm and the darkness. "May God forgive him for the falsehood !" She flung herself down in all the storm, and hid her white, despairing face in the long, daisy- studded grass, weeping for the overthrow of her hope and her love, and the desolate life that lay in ruins around her, as she had never wept before. But such a passionate burst of grief is the soonest over. She did not cry out for Heaven for ven- geance on this handsome young husband, who had deserted her at the altar, as it were, as many another might have done. "I will learn to forget him," she cried, raising her white face to the dark sky. "I will cast him out of my poor, shattered heart, as he cast me out of his, without one regret. FATEL-o-u-o-iicti-ni. °yips', the adoption of the minutes of this committee, said that Mr. Haddock was to be congratulated upon his management of the gas works during the late severs weather. , Councillor lIEPHERD seconded the motion, which was passed unanimously after a few observations from Councillors BEAMAN and SHAW.

RIVERS' COAIMITTEE.--NEGOTIATIONS WITH THE SHIP CANAL. _

Councillor ROBERTS, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of this committee, which appeared in our last issue, said he dared say they would have been amused, as he had been, at the sonsevvhat hysterical out- bursts which they had seen during the past week both at Warrington and Manchester. Oa the one hand a shareholder of the Manchester Ship Canal Company grumbled very severely indeed at what he called the bad usage of the Ship Canal Company by the Corpora- tion of Warrington. On the other hand they found one of their local newspapers becoming quite hysterical at the idea that the Rivers' Committee, and possibly ,the Corporation of Warrington, were going to give away the interests of the town to the Ship Canal Company,so that the commercial prosperity of Warrington might become a thing of the past. Between these two exactly contrary opinions he thought the committee might fl Ater itself that it was doing exactly the right thing. (11‘iar, hear.) But when he read in a paper that there were "sinister rumours" abroad about the action of the committee, he was led to consider what was the meaning of the word "sinister." Looking at the dictionary, he found that it meant" unfair, dishonest, unlucky, or inauspicious." He was quite prepared to accept that definition of the term, and to deny that it was in any way applicable to the attitude of the committee. He 'would only further say that they were now coming to close quarters indeed. wits the Ship Canal Company, and that definite action would be taken within a very short period as the result of a conference to be held within the next few days. _

(Hear, hear.) He would assure the Council, and through the Council the town of Warrington, tnat no concession of any kind whatever had been made by the Corporation to the Canal Company except what had had the support and agreement of the traders of Warrington, after due consultation with them. No deviation from the obliga- tions of the Company undsr Act of Parliament, no con- cession, no alteration, would 'cc even recommended by committee until it had the support and sanction of the Corporation and had been submitted for approval to the traders of Warrington through their accredited repre- sentatives.

--...,... Alderman BOLTON seconded the resolution. It might, he said, be ,left to the Rivers' Committee to do their best I for Warrington and for the traders of Warrington. Councillor R. T. FAIRCLOII6H said it had been said that I Warrington and the traders of Warrington wanted to I " squeeze "the Ship Canal Company. It seemed to him ! that Mr. Bythell had either ignored or wilfully miscon- I strued the action of the Warring,ten Corporation up to ' this time. For what had happened ?In the first place, ' the Warrington Corporation applied 'to Parliament to bring in a bill to improve their river in order to give it a depth of Bft. and for charging .I.d. a ton. But instead of that they came to an agreement with Mr. Adamson to lot the Ship Canal Bilrpass. Then they came to a time when the canal was nearly completed. Members of the Council would be aware that there were several turn-bridges fixed in Latchford. One of them the Com- pany got power to replace by a high-level bridge. Tile Gas Committee saw it would be a gi eat saving of money I to the Company to carry the mains over the bridge instead of under the canal. All they asked of the Canal Company was payment of the cost already incurred. If the Company had had to carry out the original arrange- ment the Cost to them would have been teens of thou- sands. Did this look like " squeezing" the Company ? After Mr. Walker died they called the attention of the Canal Company to the fact that the canal could not be opened till the river had been dredged Bft., according to Act of Parliament. Sir John Harwood came down to see them, and as the result they allowed the canal to be opened a certain length of time without putting in operation the £5O per day clause. Sir Leader Williams spoke of two or three months' grace, hut in- stead of that he had seven given to him, and they knew that the obligation had not been carried out in more than a Year. Was that "squeezing " the company? Sir John Harwood had expressed his thanks to the Cor- poration of Warrington for the courtesy they had shown. Did that indicate that the Corporation had " squeezed " the Company ? (Laughter.) It seemed to him that Mr. Bythell really did not know what he was talking about. He did not see how the Company could complain in any shape or form. The Company had had every consideration, and such remarks as those he had referred to did not conduce to bring matters to a peace- ful end. Alderman HARRISON said it appeared to him that the gentlemen connected with the Ship Canal Company were acting a most unworthy part. Their great griev- ance was that they had to carry out the solemn obliga- tions they had eratered:into. If the Corporation 'neglected to compel the company to carry out their premises the commercial interests of Warrington would suffer seriously. They were now coming to close quarters, they were told. He hoped the first step taken would he to compel Vie Company to deposit with them the £6,000 representing penalties incurred by the Company by reason of their failure to carry out their engagements. Councillor Bsiatarr asked if the Company had suc- ceeded in dredging the river to the full depth re- quired. Alderman Manama : No ; nothing of the sort. Councillor BEAMAN said that if it were alleged the Cor- poration were squeezing the Company it might be fell that they went in for their pound of flesh. Surely there were engineering resources which would enable them to give its old depth to the river, ,replacing the water ab- stracted by the Company. Alderman Sorrow said the Company hal met such difficulties that it was not likely they would be very nice in their language at all times. Let it he sufficient for I the Council to know that the Rivers Committee were , doing all they could in the interests of the town. If they exercised a little patience he believed everything would I be right. If the Company, as far as they were able, I tried to fulfil their obligations he thought the Council need not trouble themselves very much by talking about ' the matter. i The resolution was passed unanimously. OTHER. COMMITTEEi. ! The minutes of the Fire Engine, Water, and General ; Purposes Committees were passed without discussion.

At the Notts Assizes; on Tuesday, before Mr. Justice I Hawkins, Edmund Kesteven, said to have been formerly a professional cricketer, was convicted of the murder of Sarah Ann Oldham, a married woman with whom he lived at Sutton-in-Ashheld, early on Christmas morning. flel was sentenced to death. A married woman named Smith:was before the magis- trates at the North London Police Court, on Tuesday, on a singular charge of obtaining money by a trick from a rent collector. On three out of four occasions when the prosecutor went to collect the prisoner's rent she palmed off on him a bright new farthing as a sovereign. He was very short-sighted, and it was only when he exam- ined his money later that he found the farthings hut no sovereigns. He then called the police to his aid, and when the prisoner had the audacity to try the same trick again he gave her into custody. The magistrates gave the prisoner time to consider whether she would be tried by a jury or take her punishment at his hands.

LITTLE SANKEY PARISH MEETING.

Mr. Tinnion said that after the very able explanation of the Chairman be would strongly advise the meeting to accept the resolution, because they bad nothing to gain by opposing it. He questioned very much whether if they did adjourn until March 14th they could decide to oppose. Mr. Greenwell here interposed, and asked whether Mr. Tinnion had any locus standi at the meeting. Mr. Tunstall said that as Mr. Tinnion was an elector, he had of course a right to speak. Mr. Tinnion (continuing) said that he had no great desire to go into the borough, and he had opposed the, measure when it came up before,but he had come td the conclusion that there was no chance of fighting successfully. • They had much to gain by going into the borough, and had nothing to lose. If they opposed the extension they would have to find the cost of opposing it. lie thought the terms were satisfactory, and were certainly in favour of Little Sankey. He had great pleasure in seconding the resolution. The Chairman strongly advised the meeting not to adjourn. lie then put his resolution to the meeting, and it was passed with only four dissentients. Mr. Thompson asked Whether he had any right to con- tinua ti attend the District Council meeting. AS' that was his first opportunity, he also took advantage of thanking the electors for electing him upon the Board of Guardians and District Council. Mr. Tunstall, in reply to the question said that Mr. Thompson was still a district councillor. In fact it was not yet certain that Little Sankey would go into the borough. On the motion of Mr. Tinnion, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the Chairman for presiding over the meeting, and for his lucid and able speech. The Chairman in reply said that he had now been eight years in that locality, and he bad always tried to further the interests of the township. He had taken a great deal of trouble in getting this matter through,fand it would be a source of great regret if anything he bad done in connection with it should ever prove detrimental to the township. He thanked ttiem for their vote. The meeting then terminates.

DECISION TO GO INTO THE BOROUGH. , A meeting of the parochial electors of Little Sankey was 'held on Thursday evening last week in the Wesleyan schoolroom, to consider the terms offered by the Corpor- ation of Warrington' subject to which it is proposed to extend the municipal borough of Warrington so as to include the township of Little Sankey, and to pass such resolution thereon as might be deemed advisable.—Mr. R. B. Matthews, chairman of the Parish Counciapresided over, the meeting, and among others present were Messrs. J. Corredine, B. Grimshaw, S. Williamson, and Greenell (parish councillors), G. Thompson (district councillor), D. Tinnion, W. H. L. Gorse,and Mr. T. Turistall (clerk). There was a moderate attendance. After Mr. Tunstall had read the notice convening the meeting, and also the minutes of the preceding meeting, The Chairman said that before he brought his resolu- tion forward, he would explain what the Parish Council had done in the matter. Of course, the first thing that occurred was the notice from the borough of the proposed extension. A letter was received from the Corporation ' asking them to meet them to receive any proposals they had to make, and also td see what terms would be ac- cepted. The Parish Council and the Corporation had entered into negotiations, but before he mentioned those he would like to tell those' present what had to be con- sidered in the matter. First of all, as regarded the posi- tion of Little Sankey and Arpley Meadows, they felt that in face of the evidence which the Corporation would be able to bring before the Commissioners on behalf of the Local Government Board, it would be very difficult for them to bring any evidence whatever to 'pre- vent their going into the borough. The whole of the evidence appeared to be going the other way. With regard to situation and locality, and other requirements, Little Sankey was part of Warrington, being simply a populous suburb of the to wn,and there could be no ques- tion that Arpley Meadows, which comprised a larzer area than Little Sankey, and which was tacked on to them, ought to be in the borough, because it was practically without police supervisien of Any sort. The Little Sankey police ought to go there but they never did. (Mr. Tinnion : Sometimes.) Not very often,he thought. If there was a prize fight, or any of those little matters which some people took an interest in, they generally went to Arpley Mead( ws to discuss them at their pleasure (Laughter.) That was not in the interest of law and order. (Hear, hear.) It was very evident that with regard to Arpley meadows, it was very necessary that it should be part and parcel of the borough. There were other considerations which induced them to think that the Corporation would make out a good case for the township going into the borough. The sewage of the district, which was still in debt—they bad to pay it off, and they were paying it off in the rates—emptied itself in the borough. It would be a serious question, if the Local Government Board at any time required the sewage to be dealt with before it went into the river. It was a matter Little Sankey could hardly stand. There was another reasoa why Little Sankey should be joined to the borough. The property put up in the township had been put up without any control whatever, and the gen- tlemen present knew the glass of property it was—some of it was scarcely habitable. (Hear, hear.) Had itbeen built under the control of a proper authority, it would never have been allowed to have been put upais it was. (Hear, hear.) Under great pressure they had been able to get the streets paved,which was a very great improve- ment to the district, but he maintained that in the building of house property, street making was essentially part of the expenditure just as the bricks and mortar. In Arpley meadows, as they would know, the Canal Company had made a new road across the mea- dows. There was no population whatever there, and the cost of lighting, watching, Szc., of that road, if they did not go into the borough would fall upon Little San- key, which was a very important matter. There was a considerable quantity of emnty houses at present in Little Sankey. There were 62 empty houses out of a total of 432. When such matters were considered, the Parish Council felt that they could not go before the Commissioners to oppose the extension. With evidence of such a strong character, it could scarcely happen otherwise than that they would be defeated. In addi- tion to all this, the County Council were sending a repre- sentative to the inquiry to support the ad ition of Little Sankey to the borough. The Rural Sanitary Authority had passed a resolution to the same effect, so that they had no great authorities at their back to support them if they decided to oppose. The large ratepayers he had been in communication with, were also more or less in favour of going into the borough, with the exception of the London and North- Western Railway Company, with whom he had not communicated. They wOuld probably fight, for they fought everything which touched their pockets. They were a rich concern, and could afford to spend money in fighting. Mr. Fs W. Mimics had in- formed him (the chairman) that the township had made decidedly advantageous terms withi the Corporation. fie had expressed himself perfectly satisfied, and he would have been there at the meeting had he not been called away to Birmingham. When the Council discovered that it would .be impossible to resist going into the borough, the question of terms came to be considered. It was better to make terms with the Cor- poration than to take their chance before the Commis- sioners. As the Corporation asked for an interview, it was decided to meet them to see what they were dis- posed to give. As a result ol that interview, at which considerable discussion took place, they arrived at an agreement, which the Parish Council decided enani- mously to sapport. Mr. Matthews then read the agree- ment, which has been published in a recent issue. Con- tinuing, he said that it was a most satisfactory circum- stance that the footpath in Liverpool-road would be tiled at once. (Bear, hear.) The playground question was one which had caused them a considerable amount of consideration, because they had such a large number of children in that district—he did not know why. (Laughter.) They really wanted a plavground,and the Corporation had agreed to provide one. Be had no doub6 that some landowner would be glad to provide land,and that Lord Lilford would be generous ie the matter. Among other matters it kad been arranged that the Corporation should pay the costs they were put to in that meeting, and also all minting, and in facts:thy costs outside actual parish work would be defrayed by the Corporation. The Council unanimously approved that a resolution should be brought before the meeting, and he would like to say that the Council was elected to re- present the working population of Little Sankey. They only acted for the benefit of the working-classes. They represented them, and practically them alone. They considered how the question of going into the borough would affect the working-classes,and they believed that it would be to their benefit to go into the borough. There would be better police supervision. All the police cases would be dealt with before the Warrington magis- trates, who he thought, were generally known as dealing justly from the Bench in all matters, except perhaps where the licensing question arose, and then they seemed to him more or less to turn the police statien into a polling station, and they all flocked down there, just as earnestly as if they were going to vote at THE ANNUAL REPORT. a Parliamentary election. BANKRUPTCY Or A LOCAL FLOUR DEALER.

A the Bankruptcy Court on Friday, James Penning- ton, 172, Wilderspool-road, flour dealer, underwent his public examination,before Mr. Registrar Nicholson. The gross liabilities of the bankrupt were £426 2s. 5d., and his assets were stated to be £63 12s. sd. The causes of failure were alleged to be owing to falling markets and buying flour in advance for the rise The bankrupt was represented by Mr. Unsworth, and Mr. Browne was also present on behalf of Mr. Carter, a creditor. The official receiver in his observations upon the case says that bankrupt stated that he had only been in business, as a flower dealer and provender mer- chant, since February, 1893; and that he commenced such business wit a capital of £4O. He had not filed the prescribed deficiency account, but said that he had not kept proper books of accaunts, and, therefore, could not file proper deficiency account. He sustained considerable lose through the Ship Canal cutting off access to his warehouse, as he had to pay 6d. extra per sack for carriage by rail, on many hundred sacks of flour. His chief loss had been through the markets falling after he had bought for the rise. Bankrupt alleged he had kept no cash book, but added that he had a record of moneys received from credit customers, but the dates of receipt were not ' recorded in any case. He had nv record of payments other titan that contained in vouchers and his bank pass book, but the bulk of such payments were by cheque. He kept no ledger showing his accounts with either debtors or creditors. He further stated that he only first became aware of his insolvency at the beginning of last October; and admitted he had been contracting debts since with such knowledge. The unsecured liabilities, appeared to be entirely trade liabilities. Bankrupt stated that his furniture had been sold under a dis- tress; and that he sold a parlour suite about January 20th for £5, and paid the money into the Bank, which was pressing him ; and with regard to a horse, lurry, and harness, he stated that he had a horse, lurry and har- ness, which he gave up.to a creditor, about January 15th, in lieu of flour, which he was to have delivered to him. He also handed him a ton of hay, and a sack of wheat, and a small weighing machine. He had paid him £153 is. on December 13th, being cash before delivery for 200 sacks of flour at 15s. 6.1. per sack. He delivered 125 sacks, and he bought the above articles in reduction of the difference. The articles were worth £32 19s. altogether. In cross-examination by Mr. A. B. Potter (assistant official receiver), the bankrupt said that he first became aware of his insolvency in consequence of certain creditors pressing for their accounts. In addition to the causes of failure stated above,he attributed part of it to ill-health, having been 'gory seriowsly ill four years ago, and from which he still, felt effects. With regard to buying in hope of rises in market,he admitted that it was pure speculation. He estimated that he had lost £2OO or £3OO in consequence of the mat kets falling instead of rising as he had expected. There never was such a time, in the flour trade as the present. His loss owing to the Ship Canal cutting off access to his warehouse, he esti- mated to be £4O. When he commenced business he had not the whole of the £4O capital to band, and he had some liabilities to meet. He admitted that he had no surplus when he commenced business, but, he was perfectly sure that he was solvent. He gave his horse, lurry and harness to Mr. Horton but could net give any satisfactory reason why be did not sell them and divide the cash between his V.lrious creditors. Be had contracted a large portion ot his liabilities since he knew he was insolvent,but that was in the hope that he would recover his position. Mr. Potter asked a large number of questions with regard to the transattion with Mr. Horton, and also with the National Provincial Bank, but expressed himself very much dissatisfied with the replies received. In reply to Mr. W. Rowlinson, a creditor, the Pankrupt denied that he told him that he was absolutely solvent in Nevem her. —Mr. Browne : Have you not been passing for a local preaeher, aad were you not preaching the day before the meeting of your creditors ? Mr. Pennington : No. V —Was not that one of the grounds upon Which you get some of your credit? No . , The hearing was adjourned for one month.

WARRINGTON PERMANENT BENEFIT BUILDING SOCIETY. ANNUAL MEETING. _

The annual meeting of the above society was held in Cairo-street Chapel on Friday. There was a good at- tendance, and the chair was occupied by Mr. J. Ken- worthy, and there were also presel.t—Mesars. R. Davies, Sargent Brayshaw, T. Critchley, J. Dewhurst, J. Critchley, F. Massey, J. Mather, P. C. Price, W. H. Robinson, C. Twiss, G. Legge, R. WARRINGTON P.S.A. MEETINGS.

ANNIVERSARY AT WYCLIFFE.

The anniversary of the Wycliffe P.S.A. was held at Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon, when the church was crowded almost to its utmost capacity. The pro- ceec.ings throughout, were of the heartiest description, and the third year of the P.S.A. was entered upon under the happiest circumstances. The chair was occupied by the Rev. John Yonge. After the usual opening cere- monies, The CHAIRMAN, in addressing the meeting, said that it was'almost incredible that two years of the P.S.A. had passed away. It seemed hardly more than yesterday, that they first started the movement in Wycliffe, and that a number of them gathered together in that church with wondering and fearing as to the success or otherwise of the movement which they were starting. But time passed very huickly, and he supposed that the older they got, the quicker it seemed to go. He thought that all of them as they looked back over the two years of the P.S.A.would do so with feelings ef the greatest thankful- ness and eneouragement. (Applause.) He could not think that anyone was so pessimistic as to regret that they had started the movement. He believed that a great many men were thankful to God, and some were not afraid to say so, that the P.S.A. had ever been commenced. He hoped that by the help, sympathy, and encouragement of those present, the new year of the P.S.A. which they had just entered upon, would be the best yet known. There were people even to-day who were trying to pick holes, and to find fault with them. He heard of a minister the other day, who spoke of the Wycliffe P.S.A. as a Sunday afternoon en- tertainment. fie (Mr. Yonge) asked whether that were so or not ("No, no.") Another minister said about the P.S.A. movement that it was one half socialism, and the other half eight hours a day. Was the t so or not ? (" No, no.") Their object was to get hold of the men, and to attempt to raise them to a Christian standard and to make their lives in this world better. (Applause.) He was glad that he had been able to get Dr. Waite there that day, and they would no doubt lee able to get something from his speech. (Applause.) Dr. WAITE congratulated them upon two years' suc- cessful work in that movement. He heard about Wycliffe P.S.A. even in Liverpool, and Lie heard nothing but what was encouraging and gratifying. (Applause.) He thought that the problem of to-day was social in- equality. That problem exercised the minds of men in every part of the world. It was a disgrace to civilization that there was on one side so much poverty, wretched- ness and misery, while on the opposite end there was a superabundance of luxury and comfort. He asked himself the question, was there no power strong enough to bring those two extremes together and reconcile I them. The only answer he was able to give to the ques- tion was, the getting the spirit of the man who went abut dein.. good. (Applause.) They accepted the truth, he taught, that there was a mediator between man and God, but there was another truth and that was that there was a mediator between man and man—the man Christ Jesus. It seemed to him that what they wanted was more righteousness, and without this, it would be imposeible to improve the social position of the land. I hey should not make the things of this life the objective principle, but the subjective principle, so that they might rise highet in the Christian life. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN announced that 738 had already en- rolled themselves for the next year. He considered tnat that was an excellent start for the new year. During the service Dr. Waite sang " The river of years," and Mr. W. H. Waite " The pilgrims of night," very effectively, and Mr. W. H. Waite also recited "The city waif," in a manner which aroused the sympathies of his audience. The services was brought to a close in the usual manner. •

QUARTERLY OPEN MEETING AT THE Y.M.C.A. The lecture room of the Y.M.C.A.. Friars'-green was crowded on Sunday afternoon when the quarterly open meeting of the P.S.A. was held under the presidency of Mr. Thomas Birchen, J.P.,who expressed his pleasure at meeting them there that afternoon because their associa- tion was aiming at practical Christianity. He congratu- lated those present upon their work and he hoped they would go on to be still more and more successful. Later on, referring to Miss Bessie Dutton, the Chairman said that all of them would call to mind some of those of the other sex who were unremitting in their labours and who did all the good they could in a quiet manner. They were glad that one whose name was a household word in Warrington was present that afternoon to give an address. .

Miss Dutton was very glad to be there especially as the last time the weather prevented her fulfilling her promise. There were, she knew, faces there that were always there and she was gl..d to remember that. They should alt be faithful and help to save someone and draw them nearer to the love of Christ to be like Him. The greatest good in life was love and referring to Love pivine—that love of God to them—she said love was the ' only coin that passed in Heaven and the wine of love might be obtained of none save he who trod the wine- press all alone. God's love was the thing she would speak of to them—the love of a Saviour. It was for all nations and lands and there was a great deal from one fellow creature to another. There was love in the hearts of men, even men who had no link with God. They did not live in the world unexposed to dangers and they knew the terrible evils and trials that beset them. God would help and keep them through all that and He would save them from all evils. She wanted to speak of this love to them because God's love was different to man's love. In her own words she spoke of Jesus Christ with all the love she could muster up but she could not depict Him as He was.—at the close of this address which Miss Dutton had delivered in a feeling manner and illustrated with anecdotes and extracts of poetry, she was heartily applauded by the audience, who had listened with great attention throughout. Early in the afternoon Miss Kent and Mr. A. Baker sang as a duet " Love Divine," in a tasteful and enure- ciathe manner, anclatterwards Miss Kent gave a skilled and sympathetic rendition of a song iii which the ex- pression is half the success, namely, " The Children's Home." Numerous hymns were sung, in which those assembled took part with unmistakeable spirit and good- will. The instrumental music was provided by the small P.S. A. orchestra, under the able conductorship of Mr. Percy Randall, while Mr. F. Hickman gave his services as accompanist on the organ.

OFTEN TIRED BUT NEVER. WEARY.

LET'S discuss this point for two minutes. Here's a man who says that at a certain period he began to feel " tired and weary." That's precisely the way he puts it in his letter. Now anybody has a right to feel tired or fatigued (it's the same thing), after labour or much exercise. It's the body's fashion of telling to hold 9, to give it a rest. It is a natural and, in health, with supper and sleep just, ahead, a pleasant feeling. But weariness l—that's different. That comes of monotony, of waiting, of loneliness. Weariness is of the mind, not of the body. But it can arise in the body all the same. If this bothers you at first, don't say, "Stuff ! " " humbug ! " but study up on it. A man may be ted and happy, but not weary and happy. For weariness means depressed spirits, and nerves all sagged down in the middle. And when you get both at once you will be wise to find out what's gone wrong. It is a short letter, this is, and we can just as well quote the whole of it. The writer says : "It was in November, 1887, when I began to feel tired and weary. It seemed as if I bad no strength left in me. Before that I had always been strong and healthy. My appetite was poor, and for days together I could not touch any food that was placed before me. After every meal that I did succeed in forcing down I had such dreadful pains in the chest and back that I was almost afraid to eat. Then there was a sharp pain around the heart, too, as though I was stabbed with a knife. "I lost a deal of sleep, and for nights together I didn't sleep at all. Then I began to lose flesh rapidly, and was afraid I was going into a consumption, Yet I kept on with my work, however, but it was a bard thing for me, because I was so weak and nervous that I trembled from head to foot. As time got on I gradually got worse and worse, and my eyes were sunken and drawn in. I consulted a doctor in Kentish Town. He gave me medicine, but it did no good. After all this I got the idea into my head that I should not recover. "One day a lady came into the shop, and noticing the state I was in, kindly asked how long I had been ill. I told her all about it, and she said, You try Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup; it has made me well, and I believe it will do you good.' "I sent for a bottle, and after taking only a few doses

- - I felt relieved. Presently my food agreed with me, and I enjoyed my meals. I could sleep better also, and by keeping on taking this syrup I soon got as strong as I ever was in my life. Since that time (now over four years ago), I have been in the best of health. I con- sider that in all probability this remedy saved my life ; at all events, it restored my healthr and life without health don't amount to much. I gladly consent to the publication of this statement, and will answer inquiries , Yeurs truly (Signed), G. VINCIZ, Shepherd's Bush-road, London, W., November 30th, 1892." Thus Mr. VincAs unfortunate experience comesto a happy end. As he has to work for a living, like most of us, heis no doubt often tired, but never weary any more. And what can be possibly be more wearisome than long. continued illness? With him, as with millions, it was the stomach that was in fault. His food entered the stomach and stopped there. So he suffered from two bad results: he received no strength from it, but he did reaeive the deadly acids and gases which the fermented stuff gave birth to. Indigestion and dyspepsia. The same old story of pain and misery, and, thank mercy, the same story of restoration and gratitude after an appeal for help had been made to good old Mother Seigel.

AN ACTRESS CONVICTED FOR THEFT.

A CASE OF INGRATITUDE.

- At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Monday morning before the Mayor (chairman), Mr. F. Monks and Mr. J. W. Hay ward, Mary Ann Gould, an attractive looking ysung person and styled as an actress, was charged with stealing foom 14, Eustace-et., sometime on Thursday the 28th of February a ladies silver brooch, white shawl and blouse, value in all 10s., the pruperty of Henry Birchall.—The prisoner pleaded guilty. Mr. Hunt (the Chief Constable) said that the com- plainant was Mrs. Henry Thrchall, 14, Eustace-at., and the prisoner gave her name as Mary Ann Gould, 18 years of age' daughter of Joseph Gould, residing at 32, King-street, 11facclesfield,and prisoner had told him that she was born in Australia. It about two minutes to 12 on Wednesday night, last week, the complainant and her husband were sitting at home when they heard someone knocking next door. They went to the front of the house and opening the door found the prisoner with a little girl who bad directed her to the house in question. Mr. Birchall asked her what she wanted as the people bad gone to bed. She replied " I came into town about nine o'clock to night and 1 am looking for apartments ; I have a brother employed at the Theatre Rayal in ' A Day Will Come.' She had a parcel with her, and as she appeared respectable Mr. and Mrs. Birchall, seeing it was 12 o'clock told her they had a spare bed and would accommodate her. Prisoner accepted and said that she had had a telegram from her husband who was engaged here in a piece called" the Iron Chain." She then went to bed and got up the nsxt morning at 10 o'clock and had her breakfast. Mrs. Birchall had been busy in the morning to get the prisoner lodgings and bad pro- cured them for her with a Mrs. Barlow ia Nicholson-st. After some conversation about the lociglugs prisoner rose and saying "good morning " to Aire. Birchall went out without offering any payment. The latts.r thought her parcel looked a little larger and immediately went upstairs and found that her box had been ransacked and the articles producers were missing. Mrs. Birchall then went to Nicholoon-street and found the prisoner and informed her there wassomething missing :ram her house and asked to look in the parcel. This, prisoner refused in an audacious manner, but returned with complainant to Eustace-street, where Mrs. Birchall examined the parcel and found in it the shawl and blouse. The same day the matter was reported to* Detective Monaghan who, in company with P.C. Barlow, met her in Sankey- street near the park gates and recognising her from a description Monaghan said " How do you do this after- noon." Prisoner said, "I do not belong to W'arrington. I am a theatrical." Monaghan then told her why he spoke and Barlow noticed her removing the brooch which was on her clothimg. She was brought to the Psilice Station and the brooch found upon her. • Monaghan then charged her with tile thefts and she replied " Yes, sir." Inspector Spinks had since made inquiries and found that there was no such place as 32, King-street, Maccles- field, and that the prisoner was hot known there at all. —She was asked if there was anyone she could apply to and she said that her mother was in Macclesfield.—The bench eventually decided to send her to gaol for 14 days hard labours—Mr. Taylor, PC M. has since made en- quiries and found that 32 King-sa, the address the girl gave was perfectly correct and that the Macclesfield police had made a mistake in stating that it did not exist. Mr. Taylor had also received a letter from the girl's father stating 'hat he would he glad to have her back again and Mr. Taylor had made arrangements to have the girl either restored to her parents,on her re- lease or be sent to a home.
