MERE have been several impor- Parliament. Bills in tant legislative proposals laid _

before Parliament within the last few days. The Factory Bill which Mr. ASQUITH explained to the House of Commons on Friday night, marks another important step forward in the protection ef the health and limbs of the workpeople of this country. The Bill does not introduce any new principle of legislation, but in a variety of ways it will strengthen and extend the Factory Acts which have at various times been passed, by Parliament. After this Bill becomes law laundries, docks, building operations, bake- houses; and what are known as tenement factories, will be brought much more than they have hitherto been under the control of Government inspection, and there will be more stringent regu- lations with regard to the carrying on of danger- ous trades. The necessity for reforms in regard to all these matters has been abundantly proven time after time, by evidence given before Royal Commissions and in other ways and it is expected that Mr. AsOurru will have support from both sides of the House of Commons for his proposals. Among the pro- visions of the Bill are many which though not startling in themselves will work a quiet and beneficial revolution in many quarters. For in- stance, in future all operatives will have to be allowed in their working places a minimum of 250 cubic feet of air, and of 400 cubic feet when work- ing overtime, and where artificial light is used even a larger minimum space may be demanded. Further securities against fires may be enforced by the inspectors through the medium of the magistrates; and when accidents do happen in factories or workshops the Home Office may order an inquiry just as it does now in the case of coal-mines. " Out-work," that is to say, the practice which prevails in some trades of giving out work to be done at home, is to be dealt with. The practice will not be altogether pro- hibited, but when it is found by an inspector that the work is being done under unsatisfactory con- ditions, he may give notice of the fact to the em- ployer, and the employer will then become respon- sible if he continues to give out work to be done in the objectionable place. The working overtime of "young persona" will be prohibited, and the amount of time that women natty work overtime will be considerably reduced ; and at the same time it will be made illegal to give out home work to children employed in factories or workshops, or to women and young persons employed in a factory for full time, or to employ in shops women or young persons who have already done a day's work in the factory. In other more or less important details the Bill will affect the relationship of employer and em- ploy, and it will when it becomes law be another strong and useful link in the already pretty long chain of factory legislation which has been forged during the present century.

Another very important measure was brought into the House of Commons on Tuesday by Mr, JonN MORLEY, the Irish Secretary. We allude,l of course, to the now Irish Land Bill. This Bil is intended to continue the policy of Mr. GLAD- STONE'S Land Act of 1881, and to remedy some defects in that Bill which have been taken advantage of by ingenious lawyers and partial land commissioners, to the detriment of the tenants. The original Act fixed a term of fifteen years during which the tenants were to enjoy their "fair rents," but the new Act will fix the term at ten years. It has been found that fifteen years is too long a term to fix definitely in advance at a time when the price of agricultural produce fluctuates so rapidly, and all the changes, in prices for some years past have told heavily against the tenants. The Bill will also provide the tenants with better security for their improve- ments than they have hitherto enjoyed, and it will likewise seek to do something for the evicted tenants. This Bill has had a mixed reception in

Parliament. The Irish landlords and their sym- pathisers are, as usual, dead against it, but it is clear that they can not on this occasion rely upon the undivided support of the Unionist party. A serious factor in the situation so far as the Union- ists are concerned, is the fact that the Ulster farmers whether Orangemen,Liberal Unionists or Nationalists are almost unanimously in favour of Mr. MORLEY.S proposals. At numerous meetings they have passed resolutions in favour of "the bill, the whole bill and nothing but the bill," or if they have introduced any variant upon this old political battle-cry it has been in favour of making the bill even more strongly in favour of the tenant. Under these circumstances, the Union- ists are bound to pause before they unreservedly take up the cause of the landlords, and on the whole the bill stands an excellent chance of becom- ing law without serious inutilation.

GENERAL Boone who has just cow' General Booth n America. pleted a tour in Canada and the i United States, has had no reason to complain of his reception the other side of the Atlantic. His meetings have been large and enthusiastic and they have been attended by the leading citizens of the towns in which they were held. At Toronto the Chancellor of the Victorian University, who pre- sided, described the GENERAL as a modern apostle, the lineal and worthy successor of RueAnne' LOYOLA, MARTIN LI7TIIER, GEORGE Fox, and JOHN WESLRY, and even, it is said, went so far as to declare that he was greater than any of these. At Quebec,wkich is overwhelmingly Roman - Catholic, he was cordially received, and the ban- quet in his honour was attended by a number of French Catholics, including a prominent member of the Government. At Ottawa he was met by the vice-regal carriage and driven off in state to. Lord ABERDEEN'S official residence. In the United States, General Bower's visit was the- occasion of enthusiastic. manifestations, not only in centres of population where the Salvation Army is strong, but also at the Universities of Harvard, Chicago and Boston. It would appear from an interview which is published in the London "Daily Chronicle" that the main objects of General Boom's visit to the western world were, in the first place, to establish a "full scheme of social salvation for Darkest America," and, in the second place, to make arrangements for an "over--- sea colony" in Canada. With regard to the first of these projects ho declares that the social prob- lem is practically the sante in America as in Eng- - land. America has its paupers, its tramps, and - its vicious and criminal classes in whose interest the Salvation Army has established shelters and r. rescue homes. When asked in what direction he proposed to develop his social work, the GENERAL replied, "1 purpose organising a training college for social workers for all parts of the world. Dur- ing the last few weeks we have had applications from Amsterdam, Copenhagen and Stockholm for officers to organise special work on Darkest Eng- land lines. We have already 1,159 officers wholly devoted to social work." As to the establishment of the projeeted training college, the Chief of the •'" Salvationists exhibits the same qualities of faith. and energy which have carried him so far. "1 shall," he says, "appeal for men and women of all classes of society to offer themselves for training. lam convinced that there are thousands of men and women awaiting a call to a new crusade. They want a vocation, and they do not foil called upon to preach." And in support of this- argument he instanced a case that had come under his notice iu America. At one of his meet- ings a leading judge had taken the chair, and after the meeting ho found that the judge's daughter was one of the " slum sisters " of the Salvation Army. Such cases, General BO9TII declares, are becoming increa-dngly numerous amongst the Salvationists. On the sabject of the "over-sea colony" the General was inert) reserved, though, he declares that he will yet " astonish the world with that scheme. In reading between the lines of what he has to say on this subject it seems-evident that the Canadian Government has not jumped at the idea of having colonies Of the " submerged tenth" of London established in that Dominion.

The shareholders who attended the half-yearly meeting of the Ship Canal Company last week must have heard the Chairman's speech with mixed feelings. It would be satisfactory to them to learn that the traffic on the Canal c tntinues to grow. The receipts in the Second all ot last year showed an increase on those of the first half, and this is, of course, a fact of considerable significance. It indicates that the Canal has beeu found navigable and that it is ;4radually attracting traflit% On the t),,q• band, ti•ere are some serious fivaucial difficul- ties to te-in,i; ill the immediate future, There ist Au instanoe, ;Ise iisterest to he paid on the deben- cute. leans uteiuding the large sutus advanced by the Manchester Corporation. It is said that last year's revenue of .£90,000 will have to be increased about five-fold to cover the interest on these loans and the working expenses alone. These ohligat,ons cannot, be met with the balance of capital still in hand, for that is nearly all required for other purposes. It was not explained to the meeting how the directorS intended to meet these diffieuities, but it is evid- ently expected that the Manchester Corporation will prove an indulgent creditor.

We notice that at the meeting a Mr. Leigh en- tered a" strong protest "against the action of. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SUCCESSFUL STUDENT. Mr. Arthur Handley,son of Mr. Thomas Handley,J.2., r erncliffe,Higher Runcorn, passed the recent Cambridge local examination. He is being educated at Silcoates school, near Wakefield. BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS. At the meeting of this class on Wedry-sday evening, Mr. James Leach. jun., initiated a discussion on " Mer- rie England." Mr. T. Simister occupied the chair. There was a good attendance. An animated debate ensued.

- FORTHCOMING CONTENTION AT RUNCORN. Particulars will be found in an advertisement in an- other column of a Christian Endeavour Convention to be held at Runcorn on Wednesday, in the Primitive Methodist Chapel, in connection with the Warrington and district association of the Y.P.S.C.E.

MARINERS' CHURCH, IRRELL-LANE. The sixth of a series of popular free concerts was given ia the above place by the members of the choir,on Monday evening to a crowded audience, when an excel- lent programme was rendered in grand style. The chief aim of the promoters is to make them a counter attrac- tion to the public-house. U.M. FREE CHURCH MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT Crass. At the weekly meeting of this class the Rev. J.Stafford introduced a debate on " Welsh disestablishment," and speeches were made by Messrs. Gandy, Beatty, Scragg, Fowler, and W. Cooke. Mr. F. A. Clarke presided. CAMDEN WESLEYAN CHURCH. On Saturday evening, through the kindness of Mr. P. Speakman, and Mr. F. J. Norman, the workers at the above church partook of an excellent tea. Mesdames Harrison, Atkin, Rathbone, Tomlinson and Birkett, pre- sided at the tables. The Rev. W. George presided at the subsequent proceedings, and addresses were delivered by the Rev. J. H. Tite, Messrs. J. W. Baxley, F. J. Nor- man, P. Speakman. R. H. Posnett, Parker, and Heath. DEATH OF MR. W. DARRINUTON, FORMERLY OF RUNCORN. The death of Mr. W. Harrington, formerly of Green- way-road, Runcore,took place at the residence of his son at Hanley-, on the first inst. For several years Mr. Harrington was employed as an engineer at the Runcorn Soap and Alkali Works, and afterwards became an elec- trical engineer on the steamship Majestic, belonging to the White Star Line. About ten months ago he met with an accident while engaged in his duties, and has since been incapacitated and latterly bedridden. He was 58 years of age. His remains were committed to the grave at Hanley, on Tuesday, and there were present from Runcorn Messrs. E. Meadowcroft, T. Price and J. Capper. Sr. PAUL'S BIBLE CLASS. Mr. B. Kniveston presided at the weekly meeting of this class on Sunday afternoon. There was a good attendance. The address was delivered by Mr. W. Williams. Mr. B. Crosby sang as a solo "Waft ye Angels "from Jephtha, and Mr. W. Cooke who accom- panied on the organ, gave a sole on that instrument.

_ SUDDEN DEATII OF A CHILD. The six months' old son ot Mr. Lyon, 16, Grosvenor- street, died suddenly on Tuesday night. It appeared that at about ten o'clock on Tuesday morning, the child was put to bed by its mother. She went to look at him at four o'clock in the afterneon, and found him black in the face, with every appearance of having had a fit. Dr. McKenzie was called in, but the child died at ten o'clock the same night. An inquest will be held (to-day) Friday. CAMDEN WESLIMAN BAND OT Flores. On Monday there was a very interesting and success- ful gathering in connection with the Camden We,leyan Band of Hope. The efforts of the members of the Band were very much appreciated, and consisted of solos, duets and choruses by the choir, and a number of thoroughly good recitations. The Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Society have passed a vote of thanks to Captain George P. Hardley, as Cecil, of Runcorn, for having, on the night of the ech February, while proceeding to sea by the Rock and Horse Channel, picked up two men who formed the crew of the flat,Clara, which vessel had been wrecked on the West Hoyle Bank, and the men, escaping in their own small boat, had been buffet d about by the sea, had been wet through for five hours and were almost dead with cold and exhaustion. A vote of thanks and 40s. to Mr. John Edwards, mate of the Cecil, for having gone into the Clara's punt and made ropes fast to the two men by which they were hauled on board, a work of difficulty and danger, as the Cecil being in a ballast, rolled a good deal, and the punt was in danger of being capsized by the steamer's rudder.

The milder weather which has prevailed during the week has mitigated to some extent the distress which has for so long existed in the town. The Bridgewater canal is now navigable, and in consequence the boat- people are following their usual avocations. It has, therc,fore been found unnecessary to continuo the soup kitchen at the Seamen's Institute. The Runcorn Co- operative Society supplied about 800 persons with a dinner on Sunday last. Free breakfasts are still being given at the Greenwayqoad Board schools, National schools, Church-st., and at the Wesleyan schools. At these latter schools dinners have also been provided. The subscriptions received during the week include : : United Alkali Co., £lO per week to end of month ' • Mr. T. Walker, £5 ; Messrs. Reckitt and Sons, 10s. 6d. ; and Messrs. W. and R. Jacob, 104. The last of the free teas organised by Miss Preston and her sister for poor , children was held on Monday evening in Trinity school- room. About 100 children were present. The committee met eight times, and orders have been granted on various local tradesmen for food and coal amounting to the sum of £lO3, which had been cheerfully contributed by those who look after the inter- ests of the poorer members of the community. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 9, 1895.

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held at the Town Hall, Runcorn, on Thursday morning. Mr. E. Imison, JP., occupied the chair, and there were also present: Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., F. Wiswall, H. J. Grine, J. Pritchard, R. H. Posnett, D. Bisbrewn, G. H. Kirkham, J. Wilson, G. Hewitt, T. Dean, W. M. Williams, M. Sweetman, J. W. Lightburn, Dr. Gastaldi, And Mr. F. A. Lake (law- clerk), Mr. E.Marshall (secretary), and Mr. Barker (bur- veyor). THE RADICAL oFrican's REPORT.

Dr. McDougall in his report stated that the number of deaths registered during the past month amounted to 20. Five deaths were due to bronchitis,two to senile decay, four to premature birth, and One each to heart disease, inflammation of wind pipe, convulsions, diphtheria, intestinal haemorrhage, and kidney disease. At the present estimated population the above twenty deaths snowed an average death rate of 1206. Eight were infants under twelve months. Sixty births "ere registered for the month of February and 58 for January of this year. Mr. Speakman said the report WAS very satisfactory.—The Chairman said he thought it was quite phenomenal.—Dr. McDougall said it had been for the last fourteen or fifteen months very satisfactory in- deed.—The report WAS adopted.

===== 0002601_18950309_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN LIBERAL CLUB.

"THE LIBERAL PROGRAMME AND THE LABOUR PROBLEMS."

Under the auspices of the Runcorn Liberal Club, a lecture was given in the Primitive Methodist school- room, Greenway-road, on Wednesday evening, by Mr. J. W. S. Collie, secretary of the Financial Reform Ass°. ciatian, Liverpool, on ' rhe Liberal Programme and 1 the Labour Problems." The chair was occupied by Mr. G. H. Kirkham, and he was supported on the platform by Mr. A. R. Norman, Mr. J. Clarke, and Mr. T. Dean. There was a fair attendance. The Chairman made a few introductory remarks and called upon Mr. Collie to deliver his lecture. At the outset he spoke of the importance of the subject, and stated that there ought to be no discussion on the labour problems at this date on the 3.9 th century, considering the pro- gress made in science, invention, and commerce. He referred to the fact that one in eleven of the population required relief ; one out of six deaths took place in a public institution; and one out of three over 65 years of age was living in the workhouse, so that side by side with progress they had destitution and poverty. He was going to try to show that in the Liberal programme , they could 'hnd a solution of labour problems. He asked them to bring pressure upon the Govern- ment to carry out its plans. They must begin with the lower classes and try to elevate , them, for in so doing they would beneit every class of' 1 workers. He proceeded to deal with individualism versus socialism, stating that there was no hard and fast line between the two. Private property in land was the cause of the evil and he quoted extensively from n Merrie Inland," and criticised the arguments contained in the book. If they got free access to the land then many things they were striving for would become practicable and possible. The first step for them to take was to break down the land monopoly upon which others were based. Unless they did that they could not benefit the people of this country. At present although:a labourer upon the land might pro- duce sufficient to keep himselt in comfort, he had to keep the owner, pay the parson in the shape of tithes, supply the taxes oh the Government, and then keep a share as his own. So long as they had a private owner of land they had a man who could sap up everything from the labourer. If they could break down the monopoly in land it would give an impetus to trade, wages would be raised, and the unemployed would be absorbed. They could do it by the radical programme if they had unity of action. He then referred to the allotments question and the excessive amount charged for artisans dwellings through high ground rent. He asked them to loyally support the present Government and not go in for another party, as it would throw back all great reforms. Nothing was to be gained by the splitting up of the Liberal party. (Loud applause.) A short discussion ensued. Mr. Kniveton proposed the following resolution :—" That this meeting urges the Chancellor of the Exchequer to include the taxation of land values in his forthcoming Budget." The Rev. J. Stafford seconded, and it was carried. The usual votes of thanks having been passed, the meeting terminated.

BETHESDA CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH

TEA MEETING.

A most succsssful, and perhaps the best attended annual congregational tea meeting in connection with Bethesda Congregational Church, Runcorn, was hell in the schoolroom on Wednesday evening. The platform had been decorated for the occasion with coloured drapery lent by Mr.Steele,and the decorations wereearr- ied out by the Misses Heatherineton and Mr. Evans. An excellent tea was provided,the 'trays " for which were given by :—Mr. T. Jones, Mrs. and Miss Richardson, Mrs. Forrester, Mrs. Rhodos, Mrs. Hedley, Mr. and Miss Temperley, Miss Robb, Mrs. W. Cooke, Mr. and Mrs. Ackerley, Miss Clayden,Messrs. T. Handley, W. Handley J. J. Speakman, S. Walton, J. B. Chadwick, W. H. Chadwick, C. Holt, T. Taylor, J. Lunt, Cringhall and Temple. After tea the room was prepared for the sub- sequent proceedings which were presided over by the Rev. J. Simpson (pastor). The programme opened with a pianoforte solo by Miss Forrester, and it was followed by several songs, instrumental solos, and a recitation given by Miss A. Rogers. The soloists were Miss Heather. ington, Miss Wright, Mr. Gowanlock, Mr. J. Lunt, Mr. George Lacy, and Master J. L. Lunt. A violin solo " Blue Bells " was performed by Mr. W. Lunt, and Mr. P. Cartwright provided a concertina solo entitled " Gloria." A duet " List to the Convent Bells " was sung by the Misses Temple. The accompanist was Mr. William Cooke. A dialogue with the title " His Mother-in-law " was an attractive feature of the programme and it was taken part in by the Misses Ashwoieh, fleatherington, E. and J. Stone, and Messrs. Ainsworth, Johnson, Outhwaite and E. R. Roberts.—The Rev. Samuel Handley delivered a brief address. At the outset he referred to his old associations with that place of worship. The old school, he knew so well forty years ago and more, now existed ne longer. In that Sunday school he went through the various classes and eventually became teacher. He could recall many familiar names of men whom it was once a pride for Bethesda to hold as is inambers. He then proceeded to advise his hearers to think more of duty than of privilege. He said there was a tendency now-a-days for people to think that worship twico on Sunday was once tos often. That was because they only went when they were inclined. He pointed out that it was their duty to attend divine worship and urged them te be regular in their attendance at the means of graco. —The Rev. J. Simpson stated that lie had received a letter from Mr. Chadwick who was usually to be found at such gatherings as the present. As they were all aware Mr. Chadwick had been passiog through a most severe bereavement, and with him they had shown their heartiest sympathy. The letter ack- nowledged the kindly expressions of syrnpatriy.—Votes of thanks to those who had taken any share in the work of the tea and entertainment were passed.

THE SAD DROWNING FATALITY AT

It IJNCO RN.

' THE INQUEST.

Mr. J. E. Worsley at the Police Station' Runcorn, on Friday afternoon, relative to the death oj Frederick J. Le Couteur, whose body was found in the Manchester Ship Canal at Runcorn on Thursday. The first witness called was Mr. Poilip Le Couteur, 37, Bold-street, Runcorn, who stated that the deceased was his brother. Be was 29 years of age and was a marine engineer. Witness last saw him alive a few days before Christmas. He did.not suffer from any illness that was in any way likely to cause dizziness or fainting. He thought his brother fell in the lock at the bottom of the Old-road. It was a dangerous place, but was the best way for the deceased to go to his boat. William Deeprose, innkeeper of the Queen's Head, Runcorn, said that on Christmas night he had a private supper party at his house. Thera were twelve present, including the deceased, who left his house a few minutes before four on the morning ontthe 28-h inst. He told witness that he was going home. He was perfectly, sober, and was accompanied by two of his friends, John Lunt and Herbert Nicksora. He did not see deceased again. hlin Lunt, chemical labaurer, residing at 59. Sewell- street, deposed that he had known tha deceased about five years. Be was present with him at the supper on Christmas night. They left a few minutes to four the next morning. Witness and Nickson accompanied the deceased to the Church school, Church-street. Deceased told witness that he was going home until it was time for him to go to his boat. Deceased would have to go along the pierhead to go to his packet. He thought the deceased must have changed his mind about going home, and proceeded to his boat instead. Herbert Nickson, Heath-road, bricklayer, stated that he had known the deceased about ten years. He cor- roborated the last witness's statement as to accompany- ing the deceased as far as the Church school on the morning of the 28th December. He told them that he was going home, but he thought he must have changed his mind. Samuel Brown, waterrnan, Surrey-street, spoke to finding the body in the Ship Canal near to what was called No Man's Land. P.C. Gregory stated that he was present when the body was recovered. There was one mark at the back of the head, but there were no marks of violence. He found £2 03. 3d. in the deceased's pockets, and he also had upon him a gold watch and albert with a locket attached. His dress was not disarranged, and only his hat was missing. The deceased would go along the canal bank when going to his boat. His watch was stopped at two minutes to five. A verdict of "Found drowned " was returned. The Coroner on behalf of ' the jury and himself expressed sorrow with the family in the loss they had sustained. THE FUNERAL.

TEE TECENICA.L INSTITUTE.

The Technical Instruction Committee recommended that the tender of Messrs. A. and 11. Hulse for the con- struction and erection of stone stairs at the Institute as per plan for the sum of £B6, be accepted. (This will be paid by Mripßrunner). That the Council authorise the committee to advertise for tenders for the furnishing of the Institute. They also recommended that the follow- ing subjects be taught as far as was found practicable : —Freehand, perspective geometry, building construc- tion, machine construction, physiography, electricity and magnetism, sound, light, and heat, applied mechanics, carpentry, and joinery, plumbing, theoreti- cal soap manufacture, astronomy and navigation, skip building, steam; coal tar products, mathematics, languages, shorthand, dressmaking, cooking, and laundry work.—Mr. Wiswall in moving the adoption of these minutes said he wished to eliminate the first one. The Law Clerk told him that it was not necessary to pass it. If they did so they would be obliged to advertise for tenders,but inasmuch as Mr. Brunner was paying for it, it was not necessary to pass the minute. Referring to the subjects to be taught, he said they were an increase of about one-third upon what they taught at present. Their present cost was about £2OO, but in future they would have an incothe of over £3OO, so that they would be within their means in tak- ing the additional subjects. To comply with the State regulations they wonld have to equip a laboratory, and that would cost about £lOO, which would be in addition to the £5OO for furnishing. The County Council had granted them a handsome sum and they were also giving an annual grant and they must show something for it. The State had also granted them £l,OOO, and they must also satisfy it that they at Runcorn meant business.—Mr. Wilson seconded.—Mr. Bisbro wn thought the list of subjects would dispose of the objection that they sometimes heard from some oi their friends that the Technical Institute would be a white elephant. The Technical Institute would not only be for the classes but for the masses. The children of the artisans would benefit just as much as those belonging to the middle classes.—Mr. Speakman said the County uouncil had gone on a different basis for allocating the money in their hands. He understood from Mr. Ward, that the grant to Runcorn would be £313 an against £2OO as for- merly. (Hear, hear.)—The Chairman said ho thoaght Mr. Speakman had given them some information which was pleasant for them to taear.—The minutes as amended were then passed.

THII ADMISSION OF Tlig PUBLIC. In accordance with notice given, Dr. Gastaldi moved that the public be permitted to attend the meetings of the Council. —The Chairman said he might be allewed to ' say that the standing orders of tee Council were in course of preparation, and probably if they had recog- nised that before, the natico of motion would not have been given. In addition to admitting tile principle which was being contended for, it also gave some sort of regulation which he thought they cyald not rec3gnise as unnecessary.—Dr. Gastaldi said a rule in the Local Gov- ernment Act of 1894, provided that Parish Council meet- ings should be open to the public, unless the Council otherwise direct. It therefore transpired that it was the will, intention and desire of the Act thatlliose ineetings should be open be the public. Many of them might say that it was an innovation, but it was not the Ent time a motion of teat kind had been brought before an Urban Council. In the past a great many things had been said about certain cliques, and that members were sitting in their own interests. From his experience on the Coun- cil during the last few months he must say the contrary was the CASH. 111 had great pleasure is moving his motion.—Mr. G. H. Kirkham wanted to know whether Dr. Gastaldi intended the committee meetings to be open to the public also.—Dr. Gastaldi replied in the negative. As stated in the motion, it was the Council meetings only. Mr. Kirkham seconded the motion.—Mr. Speakman had hoped that the motion would not have been seconded after the remarks about the standing orders, made by the chairman. He thought it just as well to state that those standing orders wauld include every- thing the chairman had said. Almest the very first rule of the County Council said that, " Strangers shall be permitted to attend all meetings of the Conseil and to report and publish its proceedings, unless especially ex- cluded by a vote of the Council during any portion or the whole of the meeting." He oi-ought that that meet- ing would do well to wait, and not to pass that resolu- tion until the standing orders came before them for confirmation.—Mr. Lightbure propoeoci an amendment that the motion be rejected. He was a member •f the Standing Order Cominiztee and the question of admit- ting the public would be dealt with in a more perfect order by that committee. —ln answer to Mr. Bisbrown, the Cnairnian said that the idea ot the Standin; Orders Committee was that the orders should be submitted at an informal meeting of the Canned in about a fortnight'd time at the out- side.--He would suggest that Dr. Gastaldi withdraw his motion.—Mr.Posnett : Do the Standing Orders Comm,t- tee endorse the principle of admitting the pubic ?—The Chairman continuing, said that when the Standing ' Orders were submitted, if they found that the principh was not included, it would be quite open for the meet- ing to move any addition.—Mr. Taylor seconded the amendment,and said that it was simply deferring the matter instil they had something definite to work upon. It was very easy to say,"We will admit the public," hut there was the question of accommodation to be considered It 100 people came into the rown,who was to say "Nay " to them ? They bad never had am-thing to hide from the public, aml lie h med that the new Council would be the same.—Mr. Wiswall said he should vote for the amendment, not because he objected to tile public tiring admitted, but that he thougnt the matter should tie dealt with in a sensible way.—Mr. Speak:mut said he would like to explain that while the County Council me i tings were open to the put lie they never had any outsioers present.—Mr. Once suggested to Dr. Gastaldi the adv isability ol:wiihdrawing his motien that morning. —Dr. Gastaldi sae' he would withdraw the minim ilia— much as the questionl would ha br,ii.rtit f .rward in a fortnight's time.—Mr. Kirkham that he was willing that course should i.e taken.—The matter then dropped.

THE STATE OF THE CHEMICAL TRIBE.

Mr. E. Carey, writing on the 6th inst., on behalf of the United alkali Company, in reply to a communica- tion from the Council, asking them .to re-open their works at Runcorn, as soon as possibre, said "The sub- ject was considered yesterday by our directors and they have decided to continue their donation to your distress fund of £lO per week, until the end of this month,when they hope that some of the causes of depression will have disappeared. Until general trade mends, and demand increases, they cannot hold out much hope of any sub- stantial augmentation of the output of our Runcorn works." This concluded the business.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

At the Boardroom, Town Hall, on Tuesday evening, the monthly meeting of this Board was held under the presidency of Mr. James Pritchard. There were els* present :—Revs. Canon Wood, and Dr. Gastaldi. Dr. McDougall, Messrs. T. Handley, J.P., S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., E. Irnison, J.P., and G. Moss (clerk).

===== 0002601_18950309_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
ACHOOL ATTENDANCE.

From the minutes of the School Attendance Conamittee it appeared that 55 parents had been summoned before the committee. 50 of whom appeared. Prosecutions before the magistrates were ordered in six cases. There was a decrease af 128 on the rolls, and a decrease in the average attendance of 240. This was accounted for by the distress and the severity of the weather. With regard to the children in blind and deaf institutions it was reported that considerable difficulty was experi- enced in getting the money from the parents. The tender of Mr. Yates was accepted for officers' clothing. The minutes were passed.

===== 0002601_18950309_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
_THE THIS OF ELECTIONS.

Mr.Pritchard in accordance with notice giveia,moved that the Clerk be instructed to draw the attention of the returning officer to the arrangement made in 1889 for altering the date of the triennial elections, and to request hint to take steps to render that alteration per- manent. Ile said that in March, 1889, the Board de. cided t. appeal for an earlier date for the election than Christmas week. In response to that appeal a letter was received, stating that the election could be held within fifty days from the close of the year. That car- ried the election back to November 10th. In 1892 it was expected that the same rule would hold good,but it was again altered by the authorities and fixed for Christmas week.—The 'motion was carried unanimously. SHALL THE MEMBERS BE INCRBASID ?

The Chairman further moved that it was de.oirable to have the number of members composing the Board in- creased to iiine,and that the clerk be instructed to make application to the Education Department for its sanc- tion to such increase at the next triennial election. Ho sad in moving that r•solution he did so on the ground that according to the pepulation they had not an adequte representation of the ratepayers. He could not, he said, alight on any town with a population of 20,000 with only seven representatives out of 5,000 School Boards and Attendance Committees. Further, they were not in accordance with the regula- tions of the Education department, which stipulated that five members should sit when the population was under 5,000; seven members when between 5,000 and 10,000; and nine members for populations fr-m 10,000 to 40,000. It was their duty to see that the ratepayers were fully represented. Another reason for the applica- tion was on account of the diffieulty in forming the various committees when the Board only consisted of seven members. Very frequently the attendance at the Scho-d Management Committee and the Attendance Committee consisted of only one member.—Mr. S. Taylor seconded the motioo.—Dr. Gastaldi thought the population of the town was not now 20,000. It seemed to have gone down to the same original igure it was before the Ship Canal came into existence.—Dr. McDougall thought the Chairman had hardlybeen fair to the School Manage- ment Committee. During the three years he (the speaker) had been Chairman of that Committee he was not aware that there had been any difficulty in forming [a quorum at the meetings. He also took exception to the statement that thepopulation of the town was 20,000. —Mr. Handley bpoke in favour of the motion, and said the question of increasing the number of members of the Board had been spoken of in the town for some )ears. Now that they were going t.. 4 have increased responsi- bility it was only right that they should have an increase of members.—Canon Wood said from experience he found that small com:nittees worked the best. The satisfactory results obtained at their Board and Even- ing schools was proof that the present Board had car- ried out their duties.—lt was ultimately decided on the motion of Dr. Gastaldi that the natter he fully threshed out in committee, and that the motion be again brought forward at the next meeting of the Board.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRRSPONDRNT.

The letter which was read at the meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council yesterday, from the secretary of the United Alkali Company, Limited, was not one which held out any hopes for the early resumption of work at their chemical manufactories at Runcorn. In fact,the tone of the latter seeuaed to indicate that the directors of the company themselves have not much hope of such a recommendation. But the conference held yester- day afternoon of representative:, oi the Manchester Ship Canal Company and members of the Runcorn Urban Council was much more encouraging. There is every liklihood that in a few days definite infor- mation from the Ship Canal Company respecting the Urban Council's counter proposals with refer- ence to the graving dock question, will be to hand. If rumours are to be relied upon, it is pleasing to learn that the Canal Company are not averse to the propositions of the local Council, but are likely to fall in with them, and furthermore, they are desir- •ue of commencing operations at once. This, it is to be hoped, will be the case, so that tbere may be a thinning of the ranks ef the unemployed in the town.

There was quite a long. dis—cussion at the meeting of the -Runcorn School Board on Tuesday evening, when the question of applying to the Education department, for permission to increase the number of members of the Board from seven to nine, was under consideration. Runcorn, it seems, from the regulations of the Education department, has long been entitled to have nine members on the Beard, and it was in order that the ratepayers might be more fairly represented that the question was intro- duced by the Chairman. He contrasted the number of representatives on the Runcorn School Board with the number on other School Boards in the , surrounding districts which showed clearly that Runcorn was lag itig behind in the number of n.presentatives. Ile also spoke of the difficulty experienced in getting together the committees,and considered that an increased number was necessary, in-as-much as extra work would devolve upon the Board in a short time. One would have thought that such a proposal would have Leell passed with- out, much difficulty. But it was otherwise. Dr. McDougall resented strongly the remarks which had been made with reference to the working of the School Managetnent Committee; contendtig that it was a reflection on the present Board; Canon Wood liked small committees; Hr. lrnison was anxious for more information; and Dr. Gastaldi was in favour of the matter being adjr,urned in order that it might be discussed in committee. This latter proposal was ultimately decided upon, so that the matter lies in abeyance for the present.

Bro. William Hill then proposed the 'Warrington branch of the above society, coupling with it the name of Bro. John Travis, of Warrington, who has been a continuous member for 47 years. Bro. Travis re- sponded and gave a song, after which several toasts followed, interspersed with harmony. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

At Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday afternoon the meet- ing of this board was held. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. M. Intyre,J.P. In addition t) the members of the Rural Council being present there were:—Rev Canost Wood, Messrs. S. Paylor J.P., T. Handley, J P., T. Lea, and J. Ashton (clerk) and G. Ashton the deputy clerk).

===== 0002601_18950309_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
TIO3 RIVER WIAVEII NAVIGATION.

The Clerk of the Wirral Union wrote submitting a memorial re " The Weaver Navigation Bills," in which they expressed a hope that the County Council would use every endeavour to obtain the rejection by Patna- meiat of the proposals of the Salt Union Limited, and Messrs. Brunner, Mond, and Company for the surplus revenues received by the Trustees of the Weaver Navi- gation which had been payable to the county, to be ap- plied to the reduction of rates. The effect of such pro- posals becoming law would throw increased burdens of rating on the ratepayers of the county. The writer asked the Guardians to attach their seal to the memorial. —Mr. Baxter said the County Council were taking active steps to oppose the bill. The memorial would strengthen their hands.—Mr. Parr asked what the Council had re- ceived during the last five years.—Mr. Baxter said they had received nothing.—Mr. Heywood said the Weaver Navigation had raised their tolls this year on everything but salt, and the probability was that there would be an increased surplus. The toll was increased from 2d. to 4d. per ton.—Mr. Chadwick said it was very probable that any surplus would be spent in works of construc- tion and clearing off their large debts.—Mr. Baxter said the debt was £210,000.—1t was decided that the memorial be signed on the proposition of Mr. Williams, seconded by Mr. Woodward.

===== 0002601_18950309_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
TRH RINCORN REGISTRARSHIP.

The Clerk said he had received that morning a letter from the Registrar General in which he stated that he had received the resignation of Mr. Henry Coleburn, the registrar at Runcorn. The Guardians could nominate a candidate at that meeting.—The Rev. Canon Wood moved that they nominate for the appointment Mr. Cooke who was already acting on behalf of the Board of Guardians as relieving officer at Runcorn. Mr. Cooke had, he believed, disckarged practically the duties of registrar for seine time avid he bore amongst them a high character for integrity and kindness. He thought he was speaking the minds of the Guardians of Runcorn by sating that they felt that no better recommendation could be made to the Registrar General.—Mr. T. Hand- ley said he had great pleasure in seconding the proposition. When the matter was under considera- tion about twelve months ago and it was mentioned that a vacancy was likely to take place, it was understood that Mr. Cooke would have the office. He had been deputy registrar 10 or 12 years and in the absence of Mr. Coleburn he had done the work.—Mr. S. Taylor snpported the nomination.—The recommendation was carried unanimously.

FINANCE. -2IIE RATE.

Mr. Heywood gave a report and estimate of the prob- able balance in favour of or against each township in the Runcorn Union at the end ef the current half-year end- ing Lady Day, 1895, and amounts of contributions required from the overseers of the respective townships in the Union, to meet the expenditure of the ensuing half-year ending Michaelmas. 1895. The total capital for the current half-year amounted to £9,458. and the net probable expenditure was estimated at £6,228, which showed a balance at Lady Day of £3,228. The probable expenditure during half-year ending Michaelmas, 1895. was:— £10,023. The total amount required for the ensuing half-year was this latter amount, and after deducting the probable balances in favour of the townships at Lady Day, 1895, the amount required by contribution was £7,795. The rateable value of the Union was £242,371, being an in- crease of £B4B as compared with the corresponding period last year, without taking into consideration the rateable value of the townships of Grappenhall, Latch- ford Without, and Thelwall. which was £16,758, mak- ing a total increase of £17,607. A rate of 71d. in the £ would produce £7,574, and it was recommended that a call for £7.795 be made upon the overseen, being an in- crease of id. in the £. It was neceesary to make a call of Is. 31. in the £ upon the townships of Grappenhall, Latchford Without, and Thelwall as it could not yet be ascertained what balance in favour of these townships there would be on Lady Day, 1895. It was not yet known whether the balance would be handed over by the Warrington Union to the the Runcorn Union direct, or to the overseers of the respective townships, but in either case it might be used towards paying the call. The Increase of :U. hi the £ was due to the slight increase in the county rate, an increase for relief through the de- pression in trade and to certain inevovements in the House.—The recommendations of the Finance Commit- tee were approved of.

TIIE VISITING COMMITTEE'S REPORT.

The Workhouse Master read the report of the Visiting Committee which stated that the accommodation for sick inmates had been found ample until recently, when the male side had been fully occupied. It would be re- lieved by an additional ward on the male side ot the house which would relieve the hospital by providing room for old men who were really not hospital patients. I The imperative necessity of a night nurse was referred to, and it was stated that as there was no accommoda- tion for an extra officer in the hospital without going to considerable expense in building. fhey strongly recom mended that a night nurse be appointed at such a CUM per week as would cover rations, lodging, and washing outside the Workhouse, such officer to be on duty say from seven o'clock at night until eight o'clock in the morning. The aged women had a sup of tea at three o'clock each afternoon, the tea and sugar having been kindly provided by Miss Best, of Aston. An estimate was being prepared by Messrs. Timmins and Son, of Runcorn,for a double action pump for nre extinguishing which wmuld give increased pressure of water in case of emergency and would Mg* be available for pumping sing- ly if either pump should require repairs. The rebuilding of boundary walls and pointing was in the hands of a capable committee, and the Visiting Committee pro- posed to seriously consider the condition of some of the yards.—Tho sanitary coodition of the house was very satisfactory as there was an abundant supply of pure water, the closets had been improved, and the main sewer remedied.—The report was adopted on the motion !of Mr. Handley. The. Clerk was instructed to request Mr. Davies, architect of Frodsham and Runcorn,to make a sketch of the proposed new ward and also prepare an estimate of the cost. He was also instructed to adver- tise for a nurse at a salary of P.. 50 per year.—The Master was asked to obtain samples from the contractors to the Union of the cloths proposed M be substituted for the cord. The tender of Mr. Timmins for the supply of a I new pump for the house was accepted.—A letter was I received from Miss Best, of Aston, stating that she would no longer be able to supply tea and sugar for the inmates of the house, but Mrs. Marsden bad offered to d ) so.—Mr. Handley said Miss Best was leaving the neighbourhood. She had supplied the inmates with the tea and sugar for two or three years, and be proposed that the thanks of the Board be sent to her.—Mr.Taylor ' moved a vote of thanks to Miss Marsden for her kind offer. Both propoAitions were carried. This was all the business.

DEFRAUDING THE RUNCORN RELIEF _COMMITTEE.

A WARNING TO IMPOSTERS.

At Runcorn Police Court on Monday morning, Mr. J. Brundrit in the chair, Walter Stubbs and Elizibeth Ann Stubbs, Shaw-t., were summoned at the instance of the RSV. A. J. Jameson, with having on the 9th January and other days, at Runcorn. unlawfully endeavoured to procure certain charitable contributions under false pit- tentions. Defendants pleaded guilty. Mr. F. A. Lake. solicitor, prosecuted, and stated that owing to the great distress in the town, a relief fund WhB opened, and a committee formed for the purpose of collecting contri- butions and relieving the poor in necessitous circum- stances. The committee was formed into separate ward committees, and the ward committee in the present case contisted of the Rev. A. J. Jameson, Mr. Bisbrown, Mr. Williams and Mr. Wilton. They sat every Wednesday evening in Newton ward. On the 9th January, the de- fendant, Elizabeth Ann Stubbs, came to them and applied for relief. She was told to bring her husband, which she did and they informed the committee that the man (Stubbs) was entirely out of work, and that tly had four children who were under 14 years of age. C the strength of the statement a ticket for relief to the extent of six shillngs was given, atid relief tickets were granted each week, with one I exception, until February 22nd. About this time in- formation was received which led to it being ascertained that the male defendant was in work. On the week ending January 4 he was working for the Weaver Navi- gation and received T 1 Bs. for the week. On the 11th January, two days after he had applied for relief, he re- ceived in wages £1 9s. ; January 18th £l,January 25th and February Ist not quite so much ; February Bth £1 33. ; February 15th £1 23. 3d. ; and February 22nd £1 is. Besides this there was a son working, bringing in ss. a week, and he had also a daughter working near Northwich. The committee felt it was their duty to the subscribers of the fund and to the poor whom the defendants had robbed, to bring the case before the magistrates.

11-eY.A. J. Jameson corroborated the above statement, and said the total amount given in relief to the defend- ant was £1 15i—The female defendant said she was very sorry she ever went to get any relief.---rhe Chair- man said he supposed it was one of those cases which no doubt very frequently happened, though they were seldom found out. There were perhaps others who had done the same. But that did slot not make the case any let,s against the defendants. They had been obtaining money which ought to have been given to deserving people. For them to carry on such a practice was really disgraceful, and the magistrates could not allow it Co pass unnoticed. The male defendant would be com- mitted to prison for one month, and the female clefend- ant for seven days. He hoped it would be s. lesson not only to the defendants, but to others who might be nracticing in a similar manner. He was very pleased that the Relief Committee had taken the matter up.

Heavy snowstorms were on Monday reported from North Wales and from the East Coast. In Liverpool and the district the snow which an du .n,.; t:.e mostly disappeared by midday.

THE GRAVING DOCK QUESTION AT RUNCORN.

IMPORTANT MEETING.

A conference of representatives of the Runcorn Urban Council and of gentlemen closely associated with the Manchester Ship Canal:was held on Thursday afternoon at the Bridgewater House, Runcorn, for the purpose of discussing the negotiations which have taken place with regard to the graving dock question at Runcorn. There were present on behalf of the Canal Company : Mr- Rythell (chairman of the Company) Mr. Whitworth (sec. tary), Sir Leader Williams (consulting engineer), Mr. Hunter (engineer).Mr. Marshall Stevens, Mr. W. H. Col- lier, Mr. J. Meadowcroft and Mr. F. Wiswall. On behalf of the Urban Council there were present:—Messrs. P. Speakman J.P., C.C., S. Taylor, J.P., C. 0.. E. Imison J.P., J. W., Lightburn, J. Pritchard, D. Bisbrown, J. H Kirkiaam, G. Hewitt. and Mr. F. A. Lake (law clerk.). Ile conference lasted several hours, and the pros. and cons, of the subject were fully discussed in a friendly and amicable manner. Various proposals were submitted by the ship Canal authorities, which we understand, prac- tically include the counter-proposals made to the Com- pany by the Urban Council. It was understood that a communication setting forth the company's proposals would be forwarded to the Law Clerk in a few days. These will be considered at a special meeting of the Urban Council. It was anticipated that the proposals will be of a satisfactory nature arid of immediate advan- tage te the town and trade ot Runcorn.

Their bodies were covered with vermin and they scarcely had any clothes to wear. The two defendants and four children slept in one bedroom. In an upper attic two boys aged 19 and 15 slept. The bedding was quite inadequate and very filthy. The female defend- ant told the inspector of the society,• that her husband gave her 245. a week and a son gave her eleven. The male defendant was a carter at Mr. Loot's, Warrington, but he did not press the case against him. It was against the woman that he pressed for a conviction.— Inspector McAteer gave a detailed statemebt as to his several visits to the house, corroborating Mr., Knowles' statement. One child's head was a mass of scabs, and he told the mother to see a doctor about it; but she. did not do so. The house and children were in a Most deplor- able state. Mr. Lunt told him that the male defendant was a respectable old man.—lnspector Hicks and P.C. Gregory also gave evidence.—The male defendant said he had always done his best for the children. He worked at Warrington during the week and came home on Slturday. Be had told his wife to keep the children clean.—Mrs. Stringer said she never took a glass of beer in her life. Her husbaed had been out of wok for a long time. She denied the accuracy of the statements made by the witnesses for the prosecution.—The magis- trates sent her to prison for one month, and her husband for seven days.

WIRRINOTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the ab,Ave Council was held in Bewsev Chamber, on W.Ninesday, when the chair wai occupied lw the Rev. G. F. Roberts,and there were also present—iNtessrs. W. Houghton, J. Houghton, J. Ball, J. Warburton, T. Mather, T. Halton, J. W. Knowles ; S. Sutton (clerk), W. Owen (surveyor), F. Kerroot (nuisance inspector), Dr. Sephton (medical officer).

COMPLAINT OF POLLUTION. The Clerk read a letter from the Mersey and Irwell Joint Committee complaining that the river Mersey was being polluted by authorities and individuals upon its banks by tipping snow into it. The committee had in- structed their Clerk (Mr. F. C. Hutton) to call the atten- tion of the said authorities and individuals to the serious pollution thus caused. and if they did not desist pro- ceedings would be taken against them. lIOROIJOU EXTENSION. • A letter was read from the Local Government Board containing representations in favour of the extension of the Borough of Warrington. It was decided on the motion of Mr. Ball, settoaded by Mr. Halton, that the Streets Committee with the addition of Mr. J. Flough- tou, be appointed to meet the Corporation with respect to the proposed incorporation ot a portion of F aulton- with-Fearnhead. MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. ' The Medical Officer reported that during the month. there were 44 births, and 17 deaths. The death rate was equil to 14-8 per 1,000, against 18,the average death rate ot the last three months, and 88 the corresponding month last year. The death rate from zymotic diseases, equalled 2.2 per 1,000, against 22, the average rate of the past three months, 1.7 the corresponding month last year. The cases of infectious disease reported during the month were, scarlet fever, 15 cases, erysipelas, one case, and puerperal fever, one case. The scarlet fever epidemic in Padgate appeared to be declining as most of the cases were reported in the beginning ot the month, the last case occurring on tne 13th ult.—The report was adopted.

SURVEYOR'S REPORT. The Surveyor reported that during the month inspec- tions had been made of Mr. Hindley's Houses,Mr. Ward's house, Mr. Sileock's houses and the church 11011 SP Pad- gate, the '4 Black Horse" Inn stables, Sankey and Mr. Gould's Houses, Great Sankey. Certificate of habita- tion bad been issued for Mr. Gould's two houses Great Sankey. The following plans were submitted and being in accerdance with the byelaws might he approved. The Collins Green Colliery Company, Limited seweriug at Buttonwood ; Mr. Joseph Mason, additions to house at Fearnhead ; Mr. H. Ezard, 4 houses, Padgate ; Mr. James Warburton (Dalian)), alterations to house at Sankey; Mr.W.flindley,seven houses with shop,Padg,ate. He had been asked to report upon seven Louses Nos 198 t) 208 inclusive, in Liverpool-road, Sankey Bridges, and was of opinion that the whole of the sculleries, privies, and yard walls should be taken down and re- built, that the roofs of the main block should ,ibe re- paired,and that the windows and door frames and stone and brickwork should be repointed. Nos. 73 to 87 inclusive, Howard-s'.. were in a bad condition through- out, and required a great deal of rebuilding and repair- ing. The whole of the privies and yard walls should be taken down and rebuilt, and in the main block the roofs should be repaired, much of the plasterin, of walls and ceilings should be redone, the brickwork throughout be raked and pointed, and many of the doors and win- dows should be renewed. In both sets of houses the closets should be built on the pail systems and as there was ample space of land, the yards should be extended. —The report was adopted. This concluded the business.

RUGBY.

WARRINGTON v. ROCHDALE St CLEMENTS. AN AMAZING DISPLAY OF FOOTBALL.

After along period of enforced absence, the Warring- ton men were able to take the field again on Saturday, when the the team to be tackled were the St. Clements' team from Rochdale. As a county match between Lancashire and Davonshire took place on that date, Warrington were without the servicea of Dakin who was helping the County Palatine to rout the western county. Jolley was also abaent, and Nevins' place at half-back was occupied by Bennett. The Warrington team how- ever contained Callaghan for the first time, and he gave a splendid exhibition of football. Their threequarter rank was also supposed to be strengthened by the in- clusion of Harry Bowen said to be a member, of the Cardiff first team: To give a place to this player,Moores was dropped, but as Burton was indisposed, Mlores found a place after all. I very large "gate" assembled, a great number having come to see the famous Bowen of whom great things were expected, but sad to relate, these expectations did not come off, and a more elemen- tary display of football has never been shown by any player upon the Warrington ground. Had the Warring- ton club drafted in a member of some schoolboy's team, the display could not have been worst and it is prob- able that the next time a strange footballer comes this way. Warrington will be extremely chary of taking him on. But to the game. There were about 4,000 specta- tors present as the following players commenced opera- - __

tions. Warrington : Boscow, full back; Barber, Bowen, Heesom, and Moores, three quarters ; Bate and Bennett, half basks; Turner, Taylor, Berry, Callaghan,Saunders, Barrett, Andrews and Savage, forwards. Rochdale St. Clements: L. Rigg, full back ; Butter- worth, May, Kershaw, and W. Rigg, three quarters; W. fl.inson, and Germen, half backs; Hill, Ride- hough, J. Waugh, W. Waugh, Ashworth, Tweed- dale, Watmough, and Farmer, forwards. Warrington won the toss and elected to defend the petition end,Waugh's kick off was neutralised by Bate's return which found touch at halfway. From the ensu- ing scrimmage, Bate made a snarp pass to Barber, who passed several Rochdale MM. At the right moment be passed the ball to Bowen, but this Welshmari did not appear to know what to do with it, and let it fall out of his hands. This preliminary experience of Bowen's brilliance was but a foretaste of what was to come how- ever. Fortunately in this case, the Warrington forwards werein close attendance, and by a combined dribble in which Callaghan was especially prornineat, they placed the Rochdale lines in danger but Rigg saved rather tamely. After two or three scrimmages Gorman broke away and reached neutral territory before he was col- lared by Bate. The next attack was upon Moores' wing. but that player was quite equal to the occasion, and he was loudly applauded for a dashing run and kick which sent the enemy to the quarter flag. The game con- tinued fluctuating,from the half to the quarter line,until Barber accepted a pass, end dodging through the visi• tors, after a fine run, he was within a few steps off line, when he was grassed by Gorman. This was extremely hard lines, as a try seemed certain. Warrington were now having by far the best of the game, and scrimmage after scrimmage tock place in the visitors' twenty-five. The visitors' defence however was invulnerable, and despite all the efforts of Barber, fleesorn, Moores, and Bate, no score could be made. Kershaw at length brought the prolonged attack to an end by a run to half way, where he passed to Butterworth. Boscow was only just in time to prevent a score. Moores and Heesom regained a little of the lost ground, but Ride- hough by one of his famous dribbles again put his side on the offensive. Just as Rochdale were becoming very dangerous. Bate securing the ball from the scrimmage passed to Moores who in turn passed to Heesom. This player dashed through the thick, of his opponents and alter a run almost half the length of the fi-ld was almost over, under the posts,when he was floored. This was the second dose of the cruellest of ill-luck which Warrington had experienced thus far. Warrington attacked for a considerable time, but Ridehough was again prominent by a splendid dribble which removed the scene of opera- tions to the other end. Here Smack Bate broke away and had he been well supported, something tangible might have resulted. Ridehough again dribbled down the field, Bowen failing to stop him, but Barber removed the danger. The darter was only staved off temporarily, for Ridehough got the ball over the line. Be lost pos- session, but Gorman was at hand, and put the finishing touch to a rather ftukery try. Hanson just missed land- ing a goal, the ball hitting the goal stumps. From now to half-time, uninteresting, but even play followed, and the interval arrived with the score in favour of Roch- dale St. Clement's by a try to nil. Turner recommenced operations in the last half. Kershaw replied, and a roar of laughter ensued when the ball rolled between the legs of Bowen. Had not Boseow been in attendance Warrington would have been in great danger of having another score against them. As it was they were at once penned to their lines, and on one occasion Kershaw missed dropping a goal by a few inches. Boscow was compelled ta touch down. From the kick-out, the visiting three-quarters got possession and Boscow was again called upoo. A free kick against Warringten looked dangerous, and Hareem judiciously kicked in the direction of Bowen. That player attempted to cet7ch the ball in all the ap- proved football style, but the oval refused to be caught, and another fit of merriment was caused when it passed between his hands and threugh his legs again. The Rochdale forwards were upon him in an instant, and Boscow only just saved. Barter now dribbled away, but from a scrimmage Kershaw, got possession, and with only Bowen in the way he scored easily. Hanson failed to improve the score. All the rest of the play was in favour of Rochdale, and a most disappointing game came to an end with tee result that Warrington were defeated by two tries to nil. The store fairly represents the play. The inclusion ef the Weleh player seemed to upset the Warrington team completely. ROCHDALE Sr. CLEMENTS "A" V. WASHINGTON " .On Saturday the Warrington "A" team journeyed to Rochdale to play the St. Clements "A " team there. It was close on 4 o'clock when the team arrived at thathilly town, and meanwhile the weather had changed from sunny brightness to an atmosphere of gloom, threatening to snow. On reaching the field the ground was found to be in good condition and Schofield for St. Clements kicked off at 4 p.m., before only a small number of spec- tators. After a few minutes energetic scrimmeging a well directed shot from a Rochdale forward placed the Warrington goal in imminent danger. Another scrim- mage resulting, Rochdale broke loose and a neat bit of passing among the half backs brought the game well within the visitors 25, but a well directed rush reversed the position of the opposing teams and play settled down in the home goal. Here Warrington took their oppor- tunity and M Orin rushing the ball scored the first try of the match. Frora the place kick Carey converted it into a goal, following which the game again rolled back to mid-field, where J. Sankey, throwing off his opponents' made a brilliant run,almost the length of the field,onlybeing grassed close on the home line. This advantage gained, Warrington endeavoured hard to score, the consequent scrim- mages bringing the game nearer than ever to the St. Clement's goal. Woods just managed to clear the line, making the second try which Raedlea neatly converted. Half-time snon afterwards arrived with as much difter- ence in score as feeling—Warrington, 2 goals and grati- fication ; St. Clements, nil, and disappointment. LONDON AND NORTH-WF.ATERN V. CHESHIRE LINES COMMITTEE—I' match in aid of the distress was played on the cricket ground on Saturday afternoon. The day was very cold, consequently very few spectators turned up. The Mayor (Councillor FAirclough) was to have kicked off, hut he did not attend. The London and North-Western club was assisted by a number of men from other clubs, and therefore played a better game. The game %vas a very tame one, and the London and North-West,rn men scored four goals easily in the first half. During the second, half the Cheshire Lines men played up much better and succeeded in preventing their opponents from scoring any further. Norman, Bell. and Pod more were the best and most active among the players, they doing the most of the scoring. The game ended in the London and North-Western winning by 4 goals to nil. Although so few people attended, a large number of tickets were sold.

• IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT,

FRIDAY. THE NEW FACTORY BILL.

In the House of Commons, on Friday, amongst the Bills brought in by private members and read a first time was Sir C. Cameaon'eaßill "to put an end to the establishment of the Church of Scotland, and to deal with the public endowments thereof on the occurrence of vacancies." Mr. ASQUITH asked leave to bring in a Bill to amend and extend the law relating to factories and workshops. Be explained that it contained provisions for further safeguarding the life and health of workers in factories and workshops, for improving the sanitary conditions under which they worked, and for the prevention of accidents by machinery and fire. Power was given to the Hkape Secretary—a power he already possessed in reference to mines—to direct inquiry by experts into any accident in a factory or a workshop. There were also provisions to prevent an occupier evading his legal obligations by getting work dme outside in places into which inspectors could not penetrate, and to restrict the working of overtime.l There was no extension of the minimum age of child labour, but if there was anything like a generaleagree- reent in the House to adopt the age of twelve instead of eleven the Government would heartily sympathise with that view. Steam laundries would in future be treated as facteries, and other laundries as workshops ; and docks, wharves, and quays, and building operations where machinery was employed would be brought within the operation of the law, and also bakehouses generally. The "particulars clause" of the Act of 1891 was to be applied to every textile worker paid by the piece. Places where garments were made would have in future to be kept at a temperature of not less than 80 degress, and places where poisonous materials were used were also dealt with. Lastly, factories and workshops would be registered. Sir J. Goasx said be should propose a clause to extend the minimum age of child labour to twelve, so as to meet the national obligation entered into at the Berlin Conference. Sir H. JAMES. Mr. MATTHEWS, MT. MIINDELLA, and others endorsed the measure generally, Mr. Mum- DELLA. also approving of an extension of the age limit. Mr. Banns advocated the abolition of half-time labour and the raising of the age to fourteen, Leave being given, the hill was brought in, and it was read a first time amid cheers. Mr. ASQUITH introduced another bill to amend the Truck Acts,and it too was read a first time. The sitting of the House was suspended shortly before seven o'clock. At the evening sitting, Mr. BENN called attention to the proposed agreement between the Post-office and the National Telephone Company, and moved the appoint- ment of a Select Committee on the subject. Mr. A. Monte denied that there was any foundation for the idea that the Post-office was preparing to create a huge private monopoly. Sir W. Ilinconar having explained that the intention of the Government was to inquire into the nature Of agreement, Mr. BENN'S motion was withdrawn, and the Committee offered by the Govern- ment agreed to. Select Committees were also appointed to inquire into the question of employment for old soldiers, sailors and marines, and the laws relating to the adu!teration ,of food.

MONDAY. THE IRISH LAND BILL.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. Bevce.replying to Mr.TOMLINSON.Said the Board of Trade was doing its best to consider the disputes as to railway rates as expeditiously as possible, in order that traders might be able to lodge their complaints with the railway Commissioners. Mr. MORLEY asked leave tl introduce the Govern- ment's Irish Land Bill. He said that most, though not all, of his proposals were founded on the report of the Committee of that House. The right bon. gentleman went into detail as the provisions of the bill. With regard to the case of the evicted tenants, he made a proposal which representatives of Irish landlords had declared would prove adequate. He , proposed, with some modifications, the re-enactment of the 13th section of the Act of 1891. He himself had no sort of belief that meant the final settlement of the question, but it could do no harm, and if by the goodwill of the parties con- cerned the provision was widely operative the effect must be good. He appealed to all sober and responsible men to give a fair and careful consideration to the bill, and not lightly to fling away an opportunity of placing on the Statute-book a just and politic scheme. filr.Carasost said there were proposals in the bill which might lead to the subversion of many of the fundamental principles on which the Act of 1881 was founded. Mr. CLANCY said the bill did not add anything to the facilities already. existing for the restoration of evicted tenants. Dr. RENTOUL said there was not in the whole of Ulster a single tenant whose desires were not in the direction of this Bill. Mr. MACARTNEY said if he found that the proposals of the Government were genuine amendments of the spirit and the letter of the Act of 1881, they would fied no stern opponent in him ; but if they were directed to any fresh assault on the property of the Irish landlords he should meet them with strenuous opposition. Mr. T. W. Russer,r, spoke favourably of the Bill, though he said he would not pledge himself to the det ins. Colonel BAIINDERSON said if Mr. MORLEY would consent to some alterations :he Bill might pass. After some further discussion leave was given to bring in the Bill, which was read a first time. Mr. BRYCE moved fee leave to bring in the Concilia- tion and Arbitration Bill. The Marquis of CARUARTHEN was spea,king in opposition to the Bill at midnight, and the debate stood adjourned.

In the House of Lords the second reading of the Sea Fisheries Regulation (Scotland) Bill was moved by Lord TWERDATOUTIL It was, he said, in most respects the same as that which left that House last session. The objections to the Government bill last year were to the clauses requiring owners of mussel-beds to produce their respective titles, and the compulsory division of the sea. ' board into districts. With regard to the first point,some modification had been introduced; and with regard to the second, Scotland was treated precisely on the same lines as those contained in the English bill. The Mar- quis of HUNTLEY said the restrictions imposed by the bill were most unpopular throughout Scotland, and he warned the Government that the Scotch members would not permit it to pass the other House. The bill was read a second time and referred to a Select Committee. The Sea Fisheries Regulation (Scotland, No. 2) Bill was • also read a second time and referred to a Select Committee. TUESDAY. CONUILIATION IN TRADE DISPUTES.

In the HOW% of Commons on Tuesday evening Sir W. HARCOURT, replying to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, said he could not say on what day the 'first reading of the Local Veto Bill would he taken before Easter. On Mr. BRYCE'S motion for leave to introduce a bill to make better provision for the settlement of trade disputes Sir J. GORST said that what WAS wanted WAS that some public authority,should have power to compel parties to trade disputes to state their case, leaving such pllblic authority, if it thought fit, to express its opinion as to whether matters had proceeded to such an extremity that no remedy but war was practicable. But this bill only enabled the Board of Trade and local authorities to persuade people to establish volunteer boards of arbitra- tion and conciliation. Sir J. PEASE said the great point was what powers they were going to put into the 'ands of the tribunal. What he desired was some tribunal which had power to examine witnesses and report, for nothing terminated a strike so quickly as public opinion. Mr. MUNDELLA, while agreeing that the House ought to have an opportunity of discussing the subject, reminded the Opposition that they talked the bill nutseveral times last session.—Mr. CHAMBERLAIN said the bill must do something more than the conciliation boards had done; otherwise it would boa farce and a sham.—The motion was agreed to, and the bill was brought in and read a first time, the second reading being fixed for next

• Tuesday. THE BURIALSBILL.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday the second reading of the Burials Bill was moved by Mr. CARVELL WILLIAMS. The vital principle of the bill he said, was that consecration would not confer any right or privi- lege, or impose any disability, obligation, or liability which would not have attached to the burial ground if it remained unconsecrated, and any chapel, whether consecrated or not, would he available for all services. Mr. Totitnssott moved the rejection of the bill on the ground that due regard was not paid in it to the feelings and desires of the members of the Church of England. Viscount CRANBORNM seconded the motion for the re- jection of the bill. Mr. PuRKS said Noncon- formists had petitioned year after year for the amendment of the burial laws, but not from a desire to pull down the Church of England. Sir G. 0. MORGAN said nothing had done more, especi- ally in Wales, to strengthen the demand for the disestab- lishment of the Church than the attitude adopted by certain clergy in regard to the burial ser,ice. Sir W. BARCOURT said the bill had for its object the redress of grievances of a serious character,and he hoped he House would pause before it refused to give the measure a second reading. On a division being taken the second reading was passed by 224 to 113,a majority of 111. Mr. WILLIAMS then moved that the bill should he referred to the Standing Committee on Law, but objection being taken, the motion stood over. On the motion of Colonel LONG, the Market Gardeners'
