RUNCORN.

£3,000 ,DAMAGE.

Early on Monday morning, one of the most disastrous farm fires which have occurred in this district, took place at Parkside Parm, Aston, Preston Brook. The farm is in the occupation of Messrs. George and John Percival, and the block of outbuildings, occupying a considerable area, is situated within a short distance from the house. At mid-night on Sunday, gr. George Percival and a cow-man named Thomas Clarke,attended to a sick cow, and on their Issuing, everything seemed quiet and safe. But at four o'clock next morning, a ploughman named Thomas Massey was amazed to find a portion of the oetbuildings one mass Of flame. He im- mediately apprised Mr. George Percival, who was early on the spot.and found the wooden ehippons,brick stables and a large corrugated iron shed containing implements. to use hi; own words to our representative, "in a wild flame." Mr. John Percival and the farm labourers were aloe promptly in atter.dance, and a messenger was des- patched with all haste to Runcorn for the services of the tire Brigade. In the meantime strenuous efforts were made to release the horses and cows from their dangerous quarters, and this was done at great risk of life. Great difficulty was experienced in this work. especially with regard to the horses, and it was found necessary to tie bags over their heads to prevent them from seeing the flames. Thirteen horses were rescued in this manner, about 70 cows, and a fold of sheep. But these were by no means all the live steck in the burning buildings. Eight cows, eight horses,five sheep, 1 over 90 pies, and;about 100 hens and ducks were liter- ally roasted alive. Another pen, containing ten sheep; was fortunately saved,as were also about 30 pigs. The fire brigade arrived about 6.30, and they immediately directed their attention chiefly to the outbuildings which had not yet caught fire, and arrested the progress of the flames. Mr. William Wright, assistant superinten- dent of the fire brigade, was in charge. Superintendent Atherton and P.S. Bonfield also appeared on the scene. The outbuildings which caught fire were completely gutted. Ole of the buildings was of corrugated iron, and was 38 yards long, 24 yards wide and 28 feet in height. It contained, in addition to a large quantity of seed corn and provender, three lurries, two of which were totally destroyed and the other badly. damaged,and also a threshing machine and engine, reaping and mow- ing tackle and other farm implements. These latter articles have all been rendered unfit for use. The two brick stables, containing the horses, the greater portion of which were rescued, also provided accommo- dation in the lofts for about 300 tons of hay and straw, seed potatoes, and horse gearing. They were also used as a piece of storage by the wheelwright and black- smiths on the farm. .one of the horses rescued was badly hurt in rushing through the fire. The seed pota- toes were roasted in their " jackets," and the other articles have been rendered practically valueless. But it was in the shippons that the most serious devastation occurred, and the conflagration was here seen at its worst. In addition to providing accommodation for 48 milch cows they also sheltered a large quantity of pigs, and of these latter animals no fewer than 90 perished. A sheep pen end five sheep were also destroyed together with a pen of fowl*. It is highly probable that the damage would have been much more serious had not the wind changed in a more favourable direction a short time after the outbreak of fire. A horrible spectacle presented Itself when the fire had subsided. Whole carcases of pigs, sheep, horses and cows, some burnt, others partly so, were to be seen lying in heaps. Some of the cows seemed to have struggled violently in order to break away from the chains fastening them to their ealls. The roasted hogs reminded one of Lamb's dis- sertation on roast pig. The firemen continued to pour water upon the buildings until two o'clock when they returned home. The damage is roughly estimated to be £3,000, and almost all is covered by insurance. The origin of the fire remains a mystery. SHIP CANAL.

MORE SHIPS FOR THE CANAL. The steamers of the North German Lloyd will shortly begin running to Manchester, the first of them, tke Beiher, being expected on the 13th inst. A fortnightly service to Canadian ports will be established in April. The Wilson line of steamers, whose operations have hitherto been confined to Hull, will, in order to safe- guard the firm's Lancashire trade, shortly begin running from Manchester to various Baltic ports. The last item of interest is that the City line steamer City of Dublin— the first belonging to this line to come up the canal— will load in Manchester from Bombay and other Indian ports about the 26th inst.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

After a very long rest, the Warrington team were able to commence operations again, and renew their acquaint- ance with their supporters on Saturday. It was so long since , the first team operated in Warrington that they were becom- ing almost strangers, and therefore it was with something like grateful feelings to the weather, for its " better late titan never" improvement that football enthusiasts wended their way to the football field on Saturday. The "bill of fare" was nothing extra—only Rochdale Clements—but still anything would do after having no football at all for so long. There was consequently a very large gate to welcome the local heroes.

It was not the restart of the football season, however, I which was the only draw. A large number, including a contingent from Earlestown, had come to witness the advent of a new player, who was going to astonish the natives, and introduce a new style of play into the War- rington team. In fact, he was going to show Barber how to score, Heesom how to defend, and Burton how to rua,and Moores must stand down to give him a place. In fact, I know of one man who ought to have been at lais work that afternoon but who got off purposely to see Bowen play.

Well, Bowen did astonish everybody. tie was al- ways very busy watching the ball, but whenever it came near to him he almost invariably let the next man have it. Once or twice he managed to get it, but what to do with it he had not the clearest idea. He generally went in a bee line for the goal posts with a most peculiar canter, and, of course, going in a straight I line, alwayb meant being promptly collared.

The spectators opened their eyes at this kind of play. They did not give him up until half time. They thought that perhaps he had not had a fair chance. However, after the interval, the Rochdale men seeing where Warrington's weakness lay, commenced playing towards Bowen, and the result was bad for the local team. The new player certainly distinguished himself, ' but not in the desired way. His performances were the talk of Saturday night and the greater part of the week also. When the list of the team for to-day was put out in Mr. Toft's window, many were the queries whether Bowen was there again. Of course he was net.

I haie said enough about Bowen now, and few words will suffice for the game itself. I believe it is the first time that Warrington have suffered defeat at home at the hands of Rochdale St. Clements. The defeat of six points to nil about represents the game, but it must be remembered that Warrington were demoralised by the failure at three-quarters. It must be admitted, however, that the forwards played a very poor game, and will have to improve to withstand Wigan to-day. " Smack " Bate played a splendid game at half, but Bennett was only moderate. Three of the three-quarters played well considering that they were so hampered. Barber played a really magnificent game, and had the hardest of luck several times. Heesom also was as brilliant in the attack as he is usually in the defence, and Mooi-es also played a champion game. Boscow was as safe as ever, and it is surprising how consistent he always plays.

To-day Warrington meet their old foes from Wigan. The match, of course, loses some interest owing to it not counting as a league match, but the cherry and whites are always popular in Warrington, and a largo gate will doubtless be present. It will be remembered that the two teams met at Wigan shortly before Wigan were suspended, and that Warrington then drew with them, this being the first check in the .victorious career of the Wiganers. If the forwards will play as good a game as they did then, I do not doubt but that Wigan will return home defeated. It is the forward rank, however, where Warrington's weak point lies at present. The back divi- sion is greatly improved from that of the beginning of season, and it is to be hoped that the forwards will also be strengthened.

To meet Wigan to-day, the following team has been selected to do duty for Warrington :—Boscow,full back; Barber, Beesom, Burton, and Massey, threequarters ; Bate and Foden half backs ; Turner, Jolley, Dakin, Taylor, Berry, lioughton, Thorniley, and Callaghan,,for- wards.

The Warrington " A" team came in for a little sur- prise on Saturday when they visited the town of Roch- dale to combat the St. Clement's "A" team there. In the first place on arriving at their destination they were informed that the field was 6' a tidy way," but nothing daunted, the players set off and discolored ere long,that in addition to being "a tidy way," a consider- able amount of climbing was necessary before reaching it. First up and then up again—there was very little down in it—and after a walk of over a mile the much desired dressing-rootsT was gained by the Warrington men who were as hot and hungry as if the game had been played off. Nevertheless when the game started it was seen that they were still pretty spry and gave their opponents a busy time in the first half, which termin- ated with Warrington, two goals to nil.

The second half was of a livelier nature, the game working first from one goal and then to the other, Roch- dale getting in a goal and a try. The match would have ended in a draw but for the failure of the Rochdale goal kick from the second try, arid taking it all round th 9 game was very even and provided the onlookers with endless excitement and speculation. Another time Rochdale would do well to provide a 'bus for their op- ponents if they don't want to be accused of making their distant ground a stratagem for taking the stamina and freshness out of their visitors.

The Warrington " A "team go to Wigan to-day to meet the Wigan "A" team. At Warrington, Wigan won rather easily, but as the Wiganers have not played a single match since the beginning of November, they will no doubt be rather rusty. There will, however, be no cripples, and therefore Warrington must not be over confident.

THE CORPORATION FINANCES.

Colonel Ducat, an inspector of the Local Govern- ment Board,visited Warrington on Tuesday for the purpose of making an inquiry into the finances of the Warrington Corporation. The proceedings were of a private nature, and the inspector afterwards remarked that he had found everything satisfac- tory. , THE PROPOSED EXTRNSION OF THE BOROUGH BOUNDARY.

The Corp)ration have received an intimation from the Parish Council of Latchford Without to the effect that they are in favour of the proposals offered by the Corporation to the parish for amal- gamating it with the borough. Further, that a meeting of the ratepayers will shortly be held to confirm or reject the resolution of the Parish Coun- cil to accept the proposals of the Corporation. It is understood that most of the principal ratepayers in the Appleton township are agreeable to the Cor- poration's terms and are in favour of uniting with the borough. BURNING ACCIDENT. Nora Jennings, aged six years, and residing with her parents at 20, Chorley-street, was on Monday received into the Warrington Infirmary suffering from rather serious burns about the arms and face. The child being cold ran into the house to warm herself by the fire. She ventured too close to the bars of the grate, however, and her clothes became ignited. Her mother at the time was ill in bed,but some neighbours hearing the little girl's screams entered the house and extinguished the flames, but not before the child was badly burnt.

QUARREL BETWEEN INSURANCE • AGENTS. SINGULAR ALLEGATIONS. On Friday morning, at the Warrington Borough Police Court, before Alderman Harrison (chairman), Alderman Wright, Councillor Roberts, and Mr. S. Wright, Peter Delaney, 331, Knutstord-road,was charged with assault- ing and beating Frederick ['Urchin, 102, Hardy-street, on the 25th of February, and there was a cross-summons against Hitchin for assaulting Delaney at the same time and place. Mr. Forshaw,solicitor, appeared to prosecute on behalf of Hitchen, and before proceeding with the case said be objected most strongly and respectfully to lr. Samuel Wright adjudicating on the case as he understood Mr. Wright was an insurance agent himself, and his client had done business with him. Mr. Wright did not know of any, and thought he should sit on the bench. Continuing, Mr. Forshaw said hie client was an insur- ance agent in the employ of the Wesleyan Insurance Company, and the defendant was also an insurance agent in the employ of the Royal Liver Friendly Society. On the day in question about 7.30 in the eveoine. his client was collecting contributions in the district of Port- land-street, and on reaching the top of that street and justl turning the corner, the defendant without the slightest provocation or reason whatever, gave him a blow in the nose and knocked him down. His nose bled profusely and his eye was blackened. The defendant then ran away into a house, and he (Mr. Forehaw) had no doubt that if he had stood his ground his client would have dealt with him more summarily than by taking out a summons. Frederick Ilitchin, complainant, insurance agent, after corroborating his solicitor, said he did not know why the defendant should strike him as he had never spoken to him and did not know him. Mr. Browne asked complainant if he and Mr. Taylor, the assistant superintendent of his campany, had not been round to different houees defaming defendant's character. This was denied. Mr. Browne then asked if a week last Tuesday complainant did not meet Mr. Ridgway, of Wellington-st., and ask him if he knew Delaney. Complainant replied "yes," and Mr. Browne wanted to know if complainant did not say he was sorry for him and advised him to have nothing to do with Delaney. This was also denied. Ann Jane Clements, 21 Portland-st., said she saw the defendant run at complainant in Portland-st. on the 25th, and strike him in the face afterwards running away. Complainant did not strike defendant at all. Mr. Browne, for the defence, contended that the tale of the complainant was not plausible. He did not know the defendant, and yet the latter earne and struck him in the face saying ."This is Delaney the Liverpool rowdy " which name Mr. Browne understood the complainant , had called him. His client had been in Warrington about 18 months and was a respectable man. Hitchin had been vilifying his client all over the place and had said that Delaney had robbed his agents, and as soon as he got them to make a " hook" sacked them and took it from them. Unfortunately in England a man could not enter the police court for slander, but only the Assizes, and the costs there were beyond hia client's means, and therefore the only thing he had to do was to rebuke the offender. Mr. Browne went on to say that his client was losing business and meeting Hitchin on Monday evening said "Do you know a man named Delaney?" The answer was "No, I have only been here a month." The next query was" Do you know Delaney of the Royal Liver ?" " Yes I have heard of him,' answered complainant. Defendant then advised com- plainant to be careful about pulling his character to pieces. Complainant then said "I'll have none of your bouncing here," and struck at Delaney, but the latter avoided the blow and retaliated as stated. With regard to running away it was absolutely untrue. He (Mr. Browne) asked their worships to say that a man suffering from the indignities that the defendant was suffering, was morally justified in taking the law into his own hands. Peter Delaney, the defendant, and district agent of the Royal Liver Friendly Society, said that two years ago he purchased the book of a man who had since gone to Africa, for £2OO. From time to time he had heard of certain remarks having been made by complaisant, and with regard to the assault corroborated Mr. Browne, adding that he went into a shop to leave his book and not to run away. What had been said by the witness Clements,he denied. Annie Thompson, 8, Portland-street, said that on the 25th she heard a noise in the street and saw complainant talking to two women and saying that Mr. Delaney had defrauded a man out of some wages. This was after the assault. Mrs. Ridgway' Wellington-street, remembered the complainant and Mr. Taylor coming to her house on the 20th February and making the slanderous statements about Delaney which she repeated. The bench fined Delaney 104. and costs for the assault, and dismissed the cross-summons.

LATCHFORD PARISH COUNCIL.

THE PROPOSED BOROUGH EXTENSION. A meeting of the Parish Council for Latchford With- out was held on Thursday evening last weeB tt the resid- ence of Councillor P. Davies, the usual room not being obtainable. Mr. Davies took the chair and the other Councillors present were Messrs. J. Lee, H. Roberts, J. Richardson, W. Pierpoint, G. Nicholls, and G. Pilling (clerk). After the confirmation of the minutes of the last meet- ing the Clerk read a letter from the Local Government Board which stated that in connection with the exten- sion of the borough they had decided to instruct one of their inspectors to visit the borough in order that he might make inquiries with a view to holding a formal enquiry. The inspector would inform the Borough Surveyor as to the time and place for the inspection to take place. A letter was also read from Reginald Potts, clerk to the Cheshire Giunty Council in which he stated , that he had been requested to obtain a list of the sche- dule of the public books, papers, and documents under the control of the Parish Council, the person who had their control and how they were kept. The matter was settled by the fact that there were no books of that description A bill of the expenses accruing from the business of the Council showed a total of £3 16s. 11d., one item of which being disputed, the clerk promised to make inquiries. With reference to the borough extension the following letter, dated 13th February, 1895, was read from the Town Clerk I now beg to submit to you in writing the proposals of the Corporation with regard to the inclusion of part of the parlsh of Latchford Without in the proposed extended borough. The Corporation are prepared to place the ratepayers at the differential rate from 15 years as follows :—(a). For five years after the date of incorporation the ratepayers are to be exempt from the payment of any improvement rate.—(b) For a further period of five years the ratepayers are to be exempt from the improvement rate to the extent of of the amount.—(e) For a still further period of five years the ratepayers are to be exempt of .1- of the amount.—The rates levied in the borough, exclusive of the improve- ment rate, have during the last five years. been as fol- lows i-1890, 2s. 101. in the £ ; 1891, 24. 2d. in the £ ; 1892, 24. Bd. in the £ ; 1893, 21. 81. in the ; 1894, 2s. 10d. in the £.—Your rates for the last year were 3s. 14.1. in the £, so that the Cor- poration really offer to your ratepayers a reduction of 20. in the £ for the first five years. The Corporation will withdraw the proposals with regard to joining the remaining portion of the township to the parish of Grappenhall. The advantages to the inhabi- tants themselves on being incorperated should be con- sidered by you. Such inhabitants will have all the benefits wlelch the inhabitants in the borough now pos- sess. The streets and roads will be paved, scavenged, and watered; house refuse and nightsoil will be regularly and systematically collected ; the inhabitants will have the free use of the hospital for infectious diseases, the free disinfecting of their houses and clothing, the free use of the library, the iree Use of the fire brigade and appliances; they will get an immediate reduction in the price of pas-6. per I,oooft.—and the fees for burials at the cemetery will he reduced to one-half. Further, than this the House Surgeons of the Infirmary will, I have no doubt, extend their visiting to the out portions throughout the district. They will, moreover, have the benefit of complete sanitary surveillance and the services of an effieient staff. Again the facility of access to the Police Station and Town Hall, with a court of summary jurisdiction so close at hand, I have no doubt will be considered by you as matters not only of convenience hut direct- advantage.—Yours truly, J. Lyon Whittle."--The Chairman said he would like them to understand that it was a proposition to do away with the whole of Latchford Without on the North side of the Ship Canal. On their side of the Canal they would he worse off if the application was granted by the Local Government hoard. It was a question for them to determine whether they would oppose the Cur- oration or not.—Councillor Pierpoint said that person- ally he was in favour of that portion of Latchford Without on the North side of the Ship Canal being absorbed in the borough. Under the present arrange- ments they had not proper police supervision, and gas was too dear to make a general use of jt. He understood that there was only one officer in Grappenhall to over- look 2,000 acres. The difference between tile extremities of his beat was so great that it was impossible for any One man to look after so large an area, and as a rates psysr he would be glad to he included in the borough of Ws rrington.—Councillor Roberts, on general grounds would support the suggestion to transfer that portion of Latchford Without to the borough.—Couneillor Davies also supported the proposition because there was a large area on either side of the Canal vrhich,no doubt,would be ri quired for building purposes, which was now agricul- tin al land which did not pay. When it was built upon it should be seen that the buildings were properly sewered. Some strong objections to going over to Grappenhall or Appleton were also express:ids—Councillor Davies sug- gested that the Clerk should write to the Town Clerk in iinswer to the letter, stating that they would like further consultation about Oil rating of the agriculturaa land in Latchford Without, which it was proposed tol take over and thus come to a proper understanding-- I Councillor Pierpoint moved that that be cione.—Coun- cillor Lee seconded and it. was carried unanimously.— I Afrr''""" !1"1" dkon,s; n•i at the Town Hall on Monday evening UOKG was nest as u. oue lux Gk.., meeting.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Etxcroa (Earl2stown) :—Received.

SOCIALISM VERSUS COMPETITION. Ton take my life, • When you taka the means whereby I lice. (ihakespeare) Sir,—ln your last week's issue. Tony bumpkin under a non de plume, conceals his personality, but did not Succeed In concealing his political proclivities. His views Are diametrically opposed to those of Arthur,the dream- ing Coaservative-cum-Socialist poet, which shows that Tony isi another sort of amphibian, the Capitalist-cum- biberal-cum-Radioal individual,who fails to see the beam in his own eye, etc. lam not incliked to mediate be- tween these two freaks of modern Society, but when 'Tony gives Us what be no doubt considers his valuable and well-grounded opinion on the nationalisation of the lanii,and speaks about robbery being intended by Socia- lists, it becomes necessary not to allow him to trifle with thu quention, and to give him the information he un- doubtedly is wishful to get, concerning land nation- alisation. In the beginning, the earth is the Lord's and the ful- ness thereof—implying that land was not intended to be private property, and it is a fact that wherever it is converted to the use of one individual a robbery is com- mitted. The furthest that the aristocratic landowners can trace ba,ck their " rights" of ownership, is by Pro- (lacing the original title deeds granted to their ancestors bya king. But where did that king got the land from Pi With the help of his soldiers and the aristocracy's"noble" ancestors, who were captain over those soldiers, they took the kaki by " right " of conquest from the van- quosbed inhabitants of these realms. Later on in the English history we find that some founders of our " noble families" in the female line got large (estates for their progeny as the reward of most dishonorably spent lives, those same estates having first been confiseated on some pretext or another by the order of the king, and by means taken from the then possessor who held his little deeds as above shown from his ancestor who got them by fighting to help to steal them—so that on the whole the estates of our aristocracy are ill-gotten property, and if the land was taken to-day on bloc and manavl.by the government of the people, by the people, and for the people, no injustice would be done, but it would be a restitution to the nation of what was formerly stolen from the nation. The time is coming, and it won't be leng, when some- thing must be done to give back to the workers all their lighta which have been confiscated by various means and various methods at different times. The best thing for the owners of real estates and in fact of all kinds of property,land or factories,railways,or machinery,canals er shippinz,,would be to sell to the Government as at pres- ent composed and thereby obtain favourable terms. Let them take a pattern from the Irish landowners who sold their estates under the Land Purchase Act. The lauded gentry, through their friends, Salisbury and Balfour,got alone the market value for their estates paid to them with public money. When the people get to open their eyes, and send delegates into rarliament with a mandate from the working men electors, if land and property is paid for by the Government in order to take it in hand on behalf of the people, it will not be -) bought at inflated prices Nut at a greatly reduced rate compared with the price set on it at present. How this transition will take place no one could even conjeolure, But it is much nearer its completion than we think. Any impartial onlooker can see to-day that the capi- talists insatiable greed is working towards the utter destruction of individual commercialism. All our pluto- crats wish for is greater scope for exploitation, to create greater wealth by creating greater misery. The workers get poorer and poorer in proportion that the exploiter gets richer and richer, and yet Tony bumpkin dares to speak of robbery. Labour is only considered by capita- lists as a means to create surplus value and not as al means to creat3 use and exchange values for the benefit' of the community, and therefore will not allow to the workers access to the raw material or machinery. Surplus value, i.e., profits, rents, interest, dividends— that is what capitalists want, and though the workers could make a living for themselves the capitalists will not let them do 90 unless they can make a profit, that is to say, reap without sowing. Competition has been confined to the European nations until recent years, and the aim of the English capitalists have been to reduce,Eaglish working men to the level of their Dutch and Belgian competitors. By international federation the workers might have made a struggle for their class ; but mark now what is happening. We have now got to compete with the Hindoos the Japanese and the Chinamen. "II China," says Mr. Stapleton, to his constituents," should become a great manu- facturing country, I do not see how the manufacturing population of Europe could sustain the contest without descending to the level of thoir competitors," " Times " September 9th 1873. The wished-for-goal is no longer Continental wages but Chinese. Some English Capita- lists have got seven millions pounds sterling worth of machinery into India during the year 1394 and we see in the " Liverpool Echo" February 25th, that the Poaching makers of Oldham, Gorton, and Manchester; have not been so busy for years as they are now, hut that the bulk of their orders is for abroad. If the machine makers depended upon home orders they would have to shut up shop. This means that English capital produced by English labor, is going abroad to b., pat to work, to give employ to Japs,Hindoos,and Chinamen,to destroy English home industry, to starve the English workingmen. But wisat do the Capitalists care? They Can fiddle in their mansions when the workers are dying if starvation, Tony Lumpkin is talking of Socialists' Intended robberiess—Yours&c., A. BRUCHEMITE.

SOCIALISM; IS IT. ROBBERY? "I thank thee dew,f or teaching me that word."—Shakespeare Sir.—as I never like to lie long under an oblig,ation, and Mr. A. Bruchemite having laid me under one in your last, by enlightening my darkness upon the fallacies of my "well grounded opinion as to the nationalization of the land," it now behoves me to do likewise unto him. Up to last week I only suspected that the high sounding phrase " nationalisation of the land" was synonymous with robbery—purely and simply robbery. It is true the suspicion was a strong and a "well grounded" one—but now we have the suspicion converted into certainty. Socialism, al4 Bruchemite, now stands confessed as robbery, or a conspiracy to rob. When a man thinks of committing a crime—of doing evil in some form or other—his natural tendency is to seek for some excuse—soma palliative to rehabilitate his own lost self-esteem and extesuate his premeditated crivrie in the eyes of others. Plence, we find the Social- ists, having settled among themselves the desirability— from a Socialistic point of view—of "acquiring " land by "requisition" tell us—as an excuse which covers all the fault—that the land has been stolen before! Now it is .quits true--undeniably correct—to say that over and over again, far back into the "dim remote" of history, the land we love and live upon has been stolen, wrested ty violence from its previous passessor4, aye, even pre- istorically so, from those pre-oboriginal " owners or zupiers "—the fern natura, the wild things of the earth lich were dispossessed by man. But does this fact ens° a further robbery of land from someone else who 3 honestly bought and paid for it? I have a" well .unded" belief that it does not! f a Socialist was detected stealing a man's purse, old the magistrate exonerate the culprit or. the plea ,t possibly some of the MODPV might have been stolen ifore by somebody or other? I opine the answer to anti a plea would be "three months hard." Of course lie is quite asuppositious case! No Socialist would think picking pockets. No, no, the pickpocket is one, but be Land Natinn,lizer is another. " • fico for the ,hrase," quoth ancient Pistol " the wise convey it call." tif we mast "call a spade, a spade," this kind of 'ism must be called Land Robbery. Ince haying been stolen invalidate the title to If its -oho could say that he owned anything ? Even '-te shirt on his back might have this disab- roperty, what might not be the history of the raw I is coat or ti. 'very shilling in a man's pocket might Sing taint • for nateriala i Aid a

be ferfeit in the same way. The folly and fallaciousness of such talk is here apparent, but it suits not the pur- pose or the cupidity ot an uainstructed Socialistic prole- tariat, and the windy demagogues who mislead them, to consider things in this light. It is not to the interest of these frothy fulminators to tell their dupes the truth, or show them where they are wrong. They do as A.B. has done in his letter to you--spread a very little argument over a largo surface and mixed many heterogenous matters, and got considerably mixed himself ia the pro- cess. How, otherwise, can we account for his open and unblushing, advocacy of a policy of theft? Policy, did I call it ? Nay—even Socialistically it would be a folly ; for as ill-forgotton gain seldom does good, so the con- sciousness of being a society of robbers could not fail to have a demoralizing effect, and, as 'even Socialism has Tic t yet been proved capable of entirely eliminating all traces of human nature from its votaries, it might result in a revert to the had old game of "Every man for him• - seif, and the Devil take the hindmost." Friend Bruchemite quotes Scripture, forgetting that this is a two-edged sword. He might have gone a little oeeper into the matter and told us how very deeply " Cursed is the man who removeth his neighbour's land- marks," and how deeply aud doubly damned must he be, who not only removes the marks, but Use land itself.

Glib rhetoricians of the Bruchemite typs . are very ready tu slur over these awkward Anti- Socialigic facts, in order to dilate upon such pleasing theme,. as capital and competition, bloated aristocrats and the nationalisation of all bank- ing accounts! Down with thrift, and put a premium on pauperising extravagance! Now lam quite aware , that there is a kind of socialism void of this stigma of territorial rapacity, a system which seeks to do good to all without wrong to any. But this is not the creed of the crowd—which is still, "Divide, divide," and when we have spent all, "divide again." However much disclaimed, this is undoubtedly the essential dogma of the Socialist mob, and is one utterly repugnant to every bonest,honourable and sane mind. Socialism, as thus preseo:ed to us is a huge and dan- gerous experiment to be tried upon us as corpora vile— we to find materials and suffer whatever of loss or damage may—nay,must—enstne. The common ruck of Socialists want to play the game of "Heads I win—tails you lose" with our halfpenny—and bolt with the coin! Again, I say, it won't work.—Truly yours, TONY LUMPKIN.

THE WARRINCiTON MASTER BUILDERS AND

THE RECENT DISTRESS. you kindly convey through your valuable solumns thel thanks of the members of the Warrington Building Trades' Federation of Joiners,Masons, Plumbers Plasterers, painters and Bricklayers' Labourers to the members and others of the Master Builders' Association for their generous and timely aid in the recent distress— aid which was so willingly given although the masters themselves had to suffer loss through their jobs being stopped. Also, we are thankful for the feeling that ex- sists between masters and men, for action speaks louder , than words. We would also call the attention of the public to a very good plan adopted by the bricklayers' labourers which would, prevent a good deal of im- position. They called a meeting of their Society and made out a list cf distressed families, and as they know each an and their circumstances they can judge ac- cordingly. We hope that other societies will adopt the same plan. We also thank Mr. Welsby and others for the kindly aid they rendered on the occasion.—We re- main, yours faithfully, THE MEMBERS OF THE WARRINGTON BUILDING TRADES' FEDERATION.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The fortnightly meeting of this Council was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. J. Mclntyre, J .P., occupied the chair and there were also present: Messrs. J. Knowles, T. Baxter, C.C., J. Darbyshire, H. E. Hey- wood, T. Wright, J. Weir, J. B. Chadwick, J. Stokes, E. Aaron, E. Wallington, J. Pickering, S. Woodward, G. Priestner, J. Parr, T. Darlington, W. Bradford, C. Moun- field, J. Whitlow, J. W. Baguley, W. Turner, C. Roy- lance ; Mr. J. Ashton (clerk) ; Mr. G. Ashton (deputy clerk) ; Mr. Farrington (nuisance inspector) and Dr. Adams (medical officer). TER WARRINGTON BOUNDARY }INTENSION. The Local Government Board writing with- reference to the proposed visit of one of the Board's Inspectors to Warrington in connection with the above, said the attendance of the Birough Surveyor was requested in order that he might furnish any information which the Inspector might require in reference to the proposals of the Town Council, and not for the purpose of laying th.e case of the Town Council before the Inspector. 'The Board considered that the attendance either of the pro- moters or opponents of the sche-Ane was unnecessary on the occasion of the Inspector's visit.

THE BUDWOUTH WATER SUPPLY. A communication signed " Bate " was read. stating .that the notice convening the meeting of ratepayers , with respect to water supply of Great Budworth was not properly published and that the meeting which passed the resolution forwarded to the Rural District Council a fortnight ago was net representative. The notice was sett out on February 11th and the meeting held on February 14th. —The Chairman said he was not prepared to challenge the legality of the meeting. He believed the notice was something like what was stated in the letter. Whether there was sufficient notice given beforehand or notice given to all the residents, he was not prepared to say. There was perliapl one third or one fourth of the ratepayers present a'; the meeting. The resolution was carried by 16 votes to 14. The water question was a wider question than some people had any idea of. His idea Was that the people wanted water running over their slopstones. He would give notice that at the next meeting of the council he would move the appointment of a committee to deal with the ques- tioir.

THE QUESTION OF A 'PORT SANITARY AUTHORITY. The Altrincham Rural District Council, through their clerk, asked what action the Runcorn Council intended to take in regard to the question of a Port Sanitary Authority at Manchester. Seeing the adverse feelingS expressed by the great maj3riv of the autherities re- presented at the late conference, the Altrincham Council thought it advisable for some joint action to be taken by those authorities opposed, so that they could have the different views formulated for the Local firovernment Board Inquiry.—Mr. Wc,odward moved and Mr. Knowles seconded that the comtnunication be referred to the committee appointed to deal with the matter. THE MEDICAL OFFICIIIISHIP.

Mr. *Miami gave notice that he would move at the next meetiug of the Council the re-appointment of Dr. Adams, as medical offier of the Council. THE TARING OVER OF THE HIGHWAYS.

Mclntyre, Knowles, Hall, Darbvshire, Darlington, Parr, Baxter, Bradford, Stokes, Heywood, Woodward, Brandreth, Lewis and Williams. DR. ADAMS' TIV.PORT.

THE DAIRIES AND COWSHEDS REGULATIONS.

Mr. Woodward read the report of the committee who have considered this subject. They had decided to re- insert the provision which had been struck oat by the Local Government Board with reference to the cowsheds being 450 cubic feet air space for each cow, and should not apply to those buildings erected previous to the regulation coming into force.—Mr. Parr said the man that brought such a proposition about cowshels for- ward, knew little about cowsheds. (Laughter.) He had been among cattle all his life, and he always under- stood they should be kept warm, whereas he wanted them to be put in a place like a barn. Mr. Stokes stated that the provisions did not apply to old buildings.—Mr. Parr said the man did not know a cow from an ass. (Laughter.) The commitee's report was then adopted. CRICKET IN AUSTRALIA.

SUCCESS OF THE ENGLISH TEAM,

The final test match between Mr. Stoddart's English Eleven and a team representing" All Australia "ended on Wednesday in a splendid victory for the English team When play commenced the Englishmen, with ODO wicket down, wanted 269 runs to win. The first ball disposed of Stoddart, but when Brown and Ward became part- ners they made a victory for their side practically cer- tain. Brown played magnificent cricket, and was not Bent back until he bad made 140 runs. Ward was slightly more cautious, hut he played a grand innings. Before the pair were separated they put on 222 runs. Ward just failed to reash the coveted century. and MacLaren and Peel finished the match, the English team winning by six wickets. In this march no fewer than 1,384 runs were scorcd for the loss of thirty-four wickets.

NEWTON-IN-MAKERFIELD, URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

LIVELY MEETING. The monthly meeting of this Council was held at the Town Hall, Eirlestown, on Monday evening. Mr. C. B. F. Borron, J.?., C.C., presided, and the other eouncillors present were Messrs. Collingwood, Emmett, MacMahon, Giltnonr, Stone, simpson, Smith, Ormand, Beatty, Thompson, Evans, J. E. Worsley (law clerk), and H. Brierley (surveyor).

_ THB LATE COUNCILLOR G. RATCLIFFE. The Chairman said before they began the regular business of the Council, he desired to express his regret at the loss that Council had sustained by the death of the senior member of the Town Hall ward, Mr. Ratcliffe. The were all aware of the great time and attention Mr. Ratcliffe devoted to his duties, the result of which was recognised, as they all remembered, by the Town Hall ward returning him at the lest election at the-head of the pelt. For the last 13 years, Mr. Ratcliffe had devoted his time as a member of the Newton Commissioners, and he (the chairman) regretted deeply the loss they had , sustained. He moved "That this Council receives with ideep regret the announcement of the death of IMr. G. Ratcliffe, one of the councillors for this district, and desires to express its appreciation of his services to the district, as Commissioner and Councillor during the past 13 years, and that the Council convey their sympathy with Mrs. Ratcliffe and family in the loss they have sustained ; that a copy of this resolution be forwarded to Mrs. Ratcliffe through the Law Clerk." —Mr. Emmett, seconded the resolution. He could en- dorse all that the chairman had stated. There was no doubt at all, that the Council had lost one of its valu- able members ; and not only that, but the neighbour- hood had lost a member who would not easily be re- placed. He did not think there was any member on the Board who devoted more time and consideration to the duties lie had to discharge than Mr. Radcliffe did.—Mr. Gilmour, as a junior member of the Town Hall ward, wished to endorse all that the chairman and Mr. Emmett had stated. The motion was afterwargajeclopted. HIGHWAY AND PUBLIC WORKS COHMITTEE.—THE TOLLS QUESTION. It was stated in the minutes of the above Committee that the Surveyor submitted a statement showing the private streets in the district which require making, to- gether with estimates of the cost; after some discussion it was resolv ei that a Sub-Committee consisting of Messrs. Colhit gwood, McMahon, Beatty, Simpson, Thompson, and Gilmour, inspect and decide what streets should be first made, at an outlay of about £5,000; in the meantime, the Surveyor prepare esti- mates showing difference and cost of macadamizing and paving the roadways.—Mr. Gregory's report was read and adopted. A special report of street tolls received from residents and non-resideitts during the four weeks ending 18th February,lB9s, was read and surnmarizei as follows :—Residents :Ist week 193., 2nd week £1 33. 91.-, 3rd week £1 11. id., 4th week 193. 31., total £4 33.10 a. ; weekly average £1 04. 11id. Bd.; weekly average 153. 51.—After much discussion,and opinion expressed by the Law Clerk, it was resolved that further consideration be adjourned. Mr. Collingwood moved, and Mr. Beatty seconded the adoption of the minutes.—Mr. Evans said they were departing from all custom. It had generally been the practice to have the names of the members present, but they had not been given on the printed copy of the minutes supplied to them. The Chairman said if Mr. Evans made a suggestion that the names ought to be printed in future, he thought it would be in harmony with the view of the committee.—Mr. MacMahon said with regard to the sub-committee for road:making, they were never called together. He would like to know from the Chairman of the Highway Committee, why the sub- committee had not been called together, He also alluded to the question of tolls, and said the decision arrived at was altogether different to what was stated :in the minute.—Mr. Gilmour endorsed the remarks of Mr. MacMahon. He was on the sub-committee, but they had no notice whatever of any meeting, and if one had been held, they bad not been notified, and he thought they ought to have an explanation. With respect to the tolls, they would remember, when that question was, brought up, it was carried that tolls be abolished for residents in the neighbourhood. After it was carried the Law Clerk intimated that there might be some dim-. -culty at some future time in imposing the tolls. Since then he had been looking into the matter' and thought it was possible that the Law Clerk might be wrong. He thought if the town authority abolished the tells for a time they could put them on again if they wished. He was not aware that the resolution had been rescinded.— Mr. Emmett said Mr. Gilmour was quite right in saying that a resolution was carried, but it was carried in rather a hurried sort of manner, before the law clerk gave his opinion as to what they ought to do. He thoueht the resolution was rescinded after the law clerk's statement, and the proposition was really what is stated in the minutes. He understood some further informa- tion was required.—The Law Clerk said if any further information was required he should advise them to re- fer it to the committee. —ln answer to a further question the Law Clerk said the scale fixed a maximum. They had latitude up to the maximum fixed by the scale. As he understsod it, the resolution was as to what portion should be charged by the scale to residents and what to outsiders, and there the matter was left.—Mr. Emmett: Are we charging up to the maximum to outsiders ? The Su evever : We are not.—Mr. Emmett: Well ; there you are.—llltimately the question was referred back to the committee for further consideration, and the law- I clerk was instructed to -get all necessary information with rPgard to the tolls so as te be able to lay it before , the committee.—The Chairman said with regard to the inspection of the streets the committee had not yet been able to meet, perhaps owing to the wet state of the weather.—Mr. Cellingwoed said that was the reason.— Mr. Stones asked if the Surveyor proposed to lay down any more macadam this year. Be asked because he , knew that that work was generally done in the winter time. If he as proposing to do so, could he see his way to have it laid with the aid of a steam roller,instead of leaving it to the cart wheels to lay it down. In other places where steam rollers were in use excellent roads were made, and if the Surveyor could Wsee his way to hire this roller and get the roads done as soon as possible it would be a great boon to the people using the roads.—The sur- veyor said a steam roller had been engaged, and as soon as the weather was suitable and the roads in a fit con- dition it would be brought into use.—Mr. Emmett sug- gested that the bye streets also be rolled. There were more stones thrown and lying about than was good for one's feet and health, and he would suggest that where- ever macadam was laid down the steam roller be used. —Mr. Ormand said that was a question already before the committee. He thought the whole question of macadamising the roads was one which required con- sideratinn.—The minutes were then passed. ass AND WATER COMMITTEE.-:-SLEE AND CO. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

'The

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR). The frost has practically disappeared, but it has left behind a great amount of suffering; The fashionable visitant yclept " influenza," has come, evidently to stay, and nas claimed for its victims a large number a the inhabitants of Earlestown and Newton. lam told the doctors are kept busy, but of course, like all other business and professional gentlemen, they will not grumble very much at the unwonted inciease in business. It was thought that the severe frost would clear the district of sickness, hut it looks very much as if the bacoili can thrive as heartily in cold frosty weather, as in warm. The methods of treatment are various,which leads me to think that some few of those said to be suffering from influenza have got it very bad, in imagination only.

The meeting last week, which was called in sup- port of the nomination of Mr. J. H. Profit, for the post of councillor for Towh Hall Ward, was of a very promising and inspiring character. Mr. Profit is a native of flarlestown, and having grown with the place is fully conversant with its requirements. He is a Liberal of a progressive type, and has the advancement of his native town at heart. The con- test is to be fought on strict party lines, and if the Liberals of Town Hall Ward adhere strictly to their principles there cannot be any shadow of doubt about Mr. Profit's election. But putting politics on one side, there are other and weighty considera- tions, which should recommend Mr. Profit to the sympathies of the Town Hall Ward. We all know that there is a majority of Con-ervatives on the Council, and no matter what the subject is, they vote solidly. It is all very well to say they repre- sent not one ward alone, but every ward. Yes, we know all about that. It sounds all right but-- l'lrAv Mr. Profit will assist somewhat in restoring the equilibrium of the Council, and no doubt help to give the largest pqrtion of the population some voice in the management of the affairs of the town- ship. Therefore I say to all interested, vote solid for Barlestown and its improvement.

The Conservatives are quite jubilant, in a way, over the,prosnect of a political fight, and are posing as martyrs. They would have us believe that the Liberals have thrown down the challenge, and they (the Conservatives) can do no other than accept it, and in so many words accuse the Liberals of being the first to introduce politics into the contest. I Publicly, I admit the Liberals did show their hand first, but how comes it that the Rev. E. W. W. Kaye was nominated at the Conservative Club, as the candidate the Conservatives should support, long before Mr. Profit was mentioned. What the Liberals did they did openly,and in an open public meeting. and the ratepayers had the opportunity of either accepting or rejecting the candidate, but the Con- servatives did net give this opportunity,but planted Mr. Kaye down, and new they ask the ratepayers 1 to vote for him, without having a voice in his 1 selection.

Now with regard to the election of a Guardian, the Liberal party have done well to bring a lady to the front, and all I can say is I hope she will be successful. She will have the support of the other local members of the Board, and I do not doubt she will render valuable assistance to Mrs. Clay. By the way, I noticed last week you said the lady was Miss E. L. Greenall. This ia not correct, it is Miss Emily Greenall. sister to the organist of the Wesleyan Chapel. I just mention this for the guid- ance of voters.

The Burtonwood Parish Council have a very nice piece of business to settle, which will require all the descrimination they can bring to bear to amic- ably decide it. Close to the school in this rural parish is a house, which it tradition be true was

built for the Schoolmaster, who was to be allowed to live rent free,on condition that he educated each year three poor children, free of costs. I believe this was carried out for some time, but as the years rolled on the tenancy of the house became varied, and the school was taken dVer by a School Board. By some means or other the late Vicar got posses- sion of the house, and collected the rents, and paid the rates, and entered it on the records, as belong- ing to the Church. When the present Vicar came to the parish, he naturally claimed the house, but his claim was resisted. Proof was necessary on the part of the Trustees, but strange to say the deed could not be found amongst the parish records. The Vicar got possession of the house and received the rents, but now that the Parish Council has come into existence they are contesting the Vicar's claim, and have intimated that he must refund all the money he has received as rent. This he refuses to do, and says he will go to gaol first. The whole thing could be settled in five minutes if the deed, which should be with the parish records, could be found, but it cannot, and the Vicar holds the whip hand.

===== 0002601_18950309_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSICAL EISTEDDFOD AT

EA RLESTOWN.

In order to celebrate the anniversary of the Patron Saint of Wales, the Welsh Wesleyans, and Presbyterians of Earlestown united in holding a Musical Eisteddfod, in the Town Hall ou Saturday, the object being to raise funds for the furtherance of Christian work, among the Welsh friends. The Committee who organised the affair were Messrs. M. Watkins (chairman) Richard and John Morris, Richard Williams,John Williams,John Parry, E. T. Evans,and W. Jones,secretary,Robert Williams, assis-

taut secretary, John Williams, and the success which attended their efforts must certainly have been gratify- ing. The secretary's duties,which must have been of the

most onerous character were carried through with re- markable ability, and courtesy. The Eisteddfod corn: . . . _

rnenced at half-Vast two, the afternoon being devoted to brass band contests, and pianoforte, and choral com- petitions. The conductor was the Rev. W. Wynne- De/Vila of Bolton and the adjudicator, Rev. E. Davies,

Pencerdd Callestr, Holyyrell. Mrs. Rawlins, of Rainhill, should have filled the post of president, but a letter was received from Mr. Rawlins, as follows :—" I regret exceedingly to inform you that my wife has been claimed by the influenza .fiend, as a . .. . _.

victim, and is now confined tO her room.

There- is no

possible chance of her being well enough to leave it this week. She begs ms to express her very sincere regret at being forced to cancel her engagement to deliver the prizes at tbo Earlestown Eisteddfod meeting on Satur- day: _ _

- The Committee on receipt of this communication, at once waited upon Miss Allatt, of Newton-le-Willows, who kindly acceded to their request, and accordingly presided over the afternoon's proceedings. The Conductor (Rev. W. Wynne-Davies) in opening the proceedings regretted that Mrs. Rawlins who had kindly promised to be with them that afternoon was unable to be present. They were very pleased to say however that a young lady well known in the town, Miss Allatt, had come forward to fill the gap (applause)—a duty that she would un- doubtedly perform most admirably. He should like to say one or two words concerning their aims and objects in getting up that Eisteddfod. They had three motives in view. First, the aim of that Eisteddfod, of that musical festival, was to encourage the study of music in that district. They knew that Welsh people were noted for their musical capabilities, and for their high attain- ments in that art. Wherever they found a Welshman, they would find also that he took that love of his art to all parts of the world. It was their effort to encourage the study of music in the various parts of the country where they might happen to dwell. The second aim of that Eisteddfod was to celebrate the name of their patron saint, who was, as they all knew, St. David. They were as fond of him as Englishmen were of St. George Scotchmen of St. Andrew, Irishmen of St. Patrick, and they knew as much about him, as the other , nationalities did of their saints. Last, but not least, they were devoting the proceeds of the Eisteddfod to Christian work among their Welsh friends. There were three places of worship belonging to their Welsh friends, and some of those friends had united together to further this matter, trusting that there would be very substan- tial and very material proceeds accruing therefrom. The proceeds would be equally divided among their Welsh friends, who required no better raisers d'etre than that they prefer like all reasonable men to worship their God in the tongue and in the language of their heart. (Applause.) The competitions were then proceeded with, the first being Pianoforte solo (for children under 16 years of age), Fantasia" Jenny Jones " (S. Keaf),prize 10s. 6d. Open ...

to all comers. In this there were' 'Ave entries, but only four competed. The names of the comnetitors were- 1, Albert Woodcock, Earlestown (Excelsior) ; 2, Miss S. E. Watkins, Earlestown (Doris) ;3, Miss E. E. Profit, Earlestown (Cynaraes) ; 4, Miss Massey, St. Helens (Maria Herman). This necessarily took up some time, and at the close Mr. J. Howard gave a couple of whist- ling solos. The adjudicator, Mr. E. Davies, then gave his decision on the pianoforte competiOn. After giving some details he said the first and second came to the front, and on the whole he admired the playing of the first most. '''(applause.) Albert Woodcock, who is a pupil of Mr. E. Leyland, was• thereupon invested with the riband by Miss Allatt. The next was a choral competition for choirs not less than 20 in number, prize £5. The test piece was " Snowflake " (D. Jenkins). Two Choirs entered for this," United Choir," Earlestowu, conductor Mr. Robert Williams, and "Carmen Choir," Ashton, conductor Thomas Davies. After they had completed their task, the Glynne Juvenile Choir sang the chorus "Glory to i God," nan excellent manner. The adjudicator,in pro- nouncing upon the choral competition said the first choir (Earlestown United) had a little tendency to be rough, but on the whole it was very good. He awarded the prize to, the first choir, and Mr. Robert Williams was in- vested by Miss Allatt. The next item was a choral competition, for children under 16 years of age, the test piece being "Every twinkling star," choirs not to be less than 20 in number. Six adults allowed to assist. Prize 30s. Two choirs entered for this, " Glynne Juvenile Choir." Earlestown, and " Hardshaw-street Juvenile Choir," St. Helens. The latter choir, however, did not compete, and of course, the Earlestown choir had the field to themselves, but their singing was without doubt of a high order, their cohductor, Mr. John Morris, handling them in an excellent manner. Mr. J. Howard here gave another whistling solo "Ash Greve," for which he received an encore. The adjudicator in alluding to the juvenile choir, said, the rendering of this choir was something magnificent, but he should have had greater pleasure in hearing the two choirs. Mr. John Morris was invested by Miss Abaft, The brass band contest was the next item, the com- peting bands being " Earlestown " and "Sutton Oak." The best piece was a waltz" Night and Morn " (H. V. Kellar, Liverpool). First prize, £7, 2nd prize, £2. Amateur bands, not to exceed 24 players. The first band to play was "Sutton Oak "band, under the con- ductorship of Mr. J. Appleton, who also conducted the Earlestown band. The Adjudicator said it was the first time in his life he had had the honour of adjudicat- ing in a brass band contest, and he was very sorry only two bands competed, as he was looking forward to hear- ing some of the noted Lancashire bands. First band, soprano sharp in the opening part, horns flat, the per- formance of the waltz was good all through. Second band played exceedingly well, all the piece better in tune; little un,teady in tune in the waltz. Second part ; euphonium and baritone not steady. It was in advance of the first band, and would take first prize. (Applause.) This terminated the competitians,andthe Rev. R.Jenes of Ashton proposed a vote of thanks to Miss Allatt for her services. Mr. J. H. Profit seconded, and the motion was heartily endorsed. Miss Allatt gracefully acknow- ledged the compliment, and the meeting dispersed. A public tea was afterwards held at the Primitive Methodist schoolroom, when about 250 persons were present, the following ladies presiding at the tables, Mesdames Rees, M. Watkins, R. Owen, Dutton. J. Williams, R. William's, T. Williams, W. Jones, Baena, Nichols, Misses Ogden, Limond, Partington, Eagan, Davies, Twiss, Roberts, Jones, B. Jones, and Conway. EVENING CONCERT. The evening proceedings consisted of a concert, over which Mr. C. B. F. Borrnn presided. There was a very large attendance, but with one or two exceptions, the I quality of the vocal selections was only of a second rate order. Th. Earlestown band which won the first prize gave a selection of music, at the opening, after which, Mr. Pierce Hughes of Manchester, sang in Welsh "Yr Drwst." His other contributions ware " Madoline," and "Fy Mlodwen." Miss Jenny Parry, who was really the star of the evening, was attired in Welsh costume, and gale some excellent selections on the harp, her manipulation being at once clear and tasteful. The items were "The bells of Aberdyfi," "Adieu my native land," and " Mona Rhuddlan," and for each she was enthusiastically applauded, and had to respond to en- cores. Madame Ashworth Hughes, soprano, sang very fairly "The Moorish Maid," " Tell me my heart," and " Farmer John's Courtship." Miss Davies, contralto, of Earlestown, was in very fine voice, and gave excel- lent renderings of the songs "Sunshine and rain," and "This work-a-day world," and she received a good reception. The humour, if such it could be termed, was entrusted to Mr. Henry Holland, of Liver- pool. The juvenile choir who won the first prize in the afternoon, rendered by request, the test piece, " Every twinkling star," which they sang in an excellent man- ner. Mr.J. H. Profit, recited an original ode, in refer- ence to the Eisteddfod, composed by himself. The Rev. W. Wynne Davies (conductor), during an interval, gave an interesting address. Votes of thanks at the closed the concert were accorded the Chairman. ALL HotrsznoLD LINEN, hemmed and marked, free of charge.-1. and W. Dna-raft. Sankev-,t.. Warrlngton.—Advt. WARD, EARLESTOWN.

I THE VACANCY IN THE TOWN HALL

REV. E. W. W.-KATE'S CANDIDATURE. A meeting in support of the candidature of the ReT. E. W. Whittenbury-Kaye was held in the Town nall,on Monday evening. There was a fair attendance. Mr. J. Catterall presided, and was supported by Messrs. J Jones, P. Foden, and W. Mawdsley. • The Chairman, in opening the meeting, said be felt sure 'he was only expressing the feelings of those present when he submitted to them a vote of condolence with

i-r;.-R-a-tc-fiff—e-S-nd family in the great loss they had sus- tained. Mr. J. Jones seconded, and referred to

the relationship existing between Mr. Ratcliffe and his tenants which was always of the most friendly character.-2 The motion was adopted. The' Chairman then proceeded to refer to the object of the meeting, and said he held the Liberal party res- ponsible for the contest being fought on party lines,thev having thrown out a challenge which he felt the Con- servative patty were bound to accept. (Applause.) Mr.J. Jones said he felt sure that in the Rev. E.W.W.- . _ .

-- Kaye they had a gentleman fully qualified to perform the duties of a District Councillor. , (Hear, hear.)

, The Rev. E. W. W. Kaye at the outset made a very feeling allusion to the death of Mr. Ratcliffe, and ex- pressed great regret that the vacancy had been caused under such sad circumstances. .Referring to the various

subjects of interest in the township, he could only repeat what he had told them at the last election. He thought

_ the township as a whole' was very heavily rated, and that for some years it had been severely crippled. If it should be his good fortune to be elected, he should al- ways do what he could to relieve the rates, at the same time having regard to the necessary improvements. Among the improvements which were necessary, was more light required in several streets of the town' especi- ally in the Town H ' Hall ward • and street making. He felt sure they would agree with him. in advocating the pur- chase of a fire engine and escape for although it might be a little out of the rates, to be forearmed was to be

forewarned. He made a strong appeal to the 215 voters who supported him at the last election, and urged all those who favoured his candidature to use their best

efforts on the day of election to secure kis return. (Ap- plause.) MR. J. H. PROFIT, AND MISS GREENALL'S MEETING.

A largely attended meeting of the ratepayers of Town Hall ward was held in the Town Hall on Wednesday evening, 41 support of the candidature of Mr. J. H. Profit as district councillor, and Miss E. Greenall, as guardian. There was a large number of ladies present. Councillor E. T. Evans presided, and he was supported on the platform by Councillors Gilmour (Earlestown), Billinge (Haydeck), Messrs. C. W. Emmett, W. Blair, D. Forrester, P.Parr, T. Clare, Davies, W. Clare,Mr. G. W. Rawlins (Rahahill), G. F. Clarke, Mrs. Cook, Mrs. Parr, Miss Ashcroft, Mrs. Clay (Warrington) and the candi- dates. The Secretary, Mr. D. Forrester, read letters of apology from Mr. E. Walkden (Bryun), Mrs. Wilson (Ashton), Rev. Father Moore and Rev. J. H. Ridette (Elaydock). The Chairman was sorry that the Rev.J.H.Ridette and Rev. Father Moore had not been able to be with them that evening. However they had got plenty of speakers and he thought they should have a good meeting. They had put their hands to the plough, in fact to two ploughs, and they must never think of looking back,but go on, and see that their candidates were elected. They were well aware that at the meeting last week they made a choice of two candidates, Mr. Profit for the District Council, and Miss tireenall for the Guardians, and he thought they would agree with him that that meeting made an excellent choice, a choice that would do credit to the town, if they were only able to return their can- didates. (Applause.) He was sure that in Mr. Profit for the District Council they had an excellent candidate, a young man who was born in Earlestown, as he told them last week, and knew all the streets and roads and footpaths about the place. His opponent could not say 'so much, he was comparatively strange there, although they had not a word to saysagainst him personally. He (the chairman) hoped they would adopt the Newton tactics, and vote for their old residents. He was very pleased iadeed when he head that Miss Greenall had given her consent. She had no motive in coming for- ward in any way, except that it was a matter of public duty. (Applause.) Mr. W. Clare moved "That Miss Emily Greenall is a fit and proper person to represent this ward on the Board of Guardians, also that Mr. J. H. Profit is a fit and proper person to represent this ward on the Urban District Council, and that this meeting pledges itself Ito use all legitimate means to secure their election on the 14th March." Miss Greenall deserved great credit for coming out on that occasion. For a lady in her position it was a great sacrifice for her to do so, and he thought they could not do better than return her at the head of the poll, to help the other lady member at Warrington. (Applause.) With regard to Mr. Profit he always found him to be a genuine man. When he told them that Mr. Profit came out at the top of the list, out of 1,300 candi- dates, in his examinations for a position in Her Majesty's Custom, he thought that spoke well for his intelligence, and they could not be taunted with sending a duffer or a muff to the Council. There were enough of that kind there already. (Laughter and applause.) Mr. Profit hacTno other interest to serve but their's ; he was not an employer of labour, he was not going to represent the bottle works, the sugar works, or the Viaduct. He thought the Council was the wrong place for a parson, because a minister was set aside for a speciac duty, to look after the spiritual interest of the parish and there- fore they did not want to divorce a man from his spiri- tual work, to look after matters of that kind. He thought if thee returned Mr. Profit at the head of the poll, he would serve them very well. (Applause.) Mr. J. Latham seconded, and criticised Mr. Kaye's re- marks at the meeting on Monday night, and asked hew he was going.`tto introduce new schemes and prevent the increase of rates.—Councillor J. Billinge, of Haydock, spoke in support of the resolution,and strongly advocated the candidature of Miss Greenall, recommending them, by all means to return her at the head of the poll. He said in the Workhouse there were about 210 male inmates who had 26 direct representatives on the board, whilst the females had only one representative, and the female Inmates were expecting every elector in the Town Hall ward to do his duty, and return Miss Greenall at the head of the poll. Mr. G. W. Rawlins (Rainhill).also spoke in support of the resolution. The fact of them assembling in such large numbers on such a wet night showed they meant on that occasion thorough business, and that they in- tended to go on with the work they had begun, until it had been brought to a successful issue. Miss Greenall he had had the pleasure of knowing for some time, and he could truthfully say, from the intercourse that he had bars with her, that she was eminently fitted to reproient them on the Board of Guardians at Warrington, and to represent them in such a manner as no man could pos- sibly represent them. (Hear, hear.) Lady Guardians would raise the moral tone of the inmatee,and look after the women and children in a way that it was not in a man to do. All they had to do was to work, and if they worked there was no doubt at all about it, they would get Miss Greenall in. (Applause.) The resolution was then put and unanimously carried. Miss Greenall. in returning thank, said that for a long time, she had been convinced that women ought to be on those Boards. There were many things in connec- tion with a Workhouse, that women could attend to better than men. One thing especially she thought ought to be done, and that was to separate the respectable de- serving poor from the vagrant poor. In the sick wards she would alqi separate them, as it must be terrible for a decent respectable woman, to lie in bed in the same ward, as a woman, cursing and swearing until the last. She would advocate the distribution of literature among the inmates. She had had plenty of kind wishes from friends of both sides of politics, and if elected, she would d her beat. (Applause.) Mr. J. H. Profit in the caurse of his remarks alluded to poor people requiring medical relief, having to walk to Newton for their medicine, he thought a sub-Surgery should be opened in Earlestown where people mild get their medicine, instead of having to walk to Newton. He was in favour of a mortuary being erected somewhere near the place where the ambulance car was now located. He gave his views on footpaths, wastes, and open places and explained by what means the ratepayers could obtain allotments of land. He wanted them to work hard in that election, because a good deal depended upon it. If they obtained a victory in that election, they, might depend upon it, they would be able to commands decisive victory in the Crow-lane ward, in the coming year, and accompanying that, a more decisive victory in the Wargrave ward, and by doing that they would have the pleasure of witnessing the reconstruction and reformation of the whole Urban District Council. (Applause.) Mr. Gilmore proposed a vote sf thanks to the visitors. Rev. F. ,E. Miller seconded and it was heartily accorded. Mr. C. W. Emmett proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman, which Mr. T. Clare seconded, and Mr. W. Davies supported, and it was unanimously adopted. THE NOMINATIONS.

IT'

Tuesday was the last day for nominations, and the result having been made public, we find that the fol- lowing have been nominated :—For District Councillor —John Carlin, 22, Derby-st.; Earlestown, master sweep. —Eugene Walter Wittenburg-Kaye, 39. Cross-lane,clerk in Holy Orders.—John Henry Profit, 128, Market-street, Earlestown, clerk in Her Majesty's customs. For Poor Law Guardian.—Emily Greenall, Grove Cottage, Crow-lane, Newton, spinster.—T. R. Matthews. The Vicarage, Earlestown, clerk in Holy Orders.— Thomas Nevitt, Queen-st., Earlestown, butcher. John Carlin has since withdrawn. The polling is fixed for Thursday, the 14th inst.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The fortnightly meeting of the above Board was held at the Board room of the Workhouse, on Wednesday. Mr. W. Houghton presided, and there were also present Mrs. Clay, Rev. G. F. Roberts, Messrs. S. Davies, G. Thompsdn, J. that ke, T. B. Malkin, T. Hatton, W. Hick- man, A. J. Jolley, E. L. Bent, W. Cross, J. Houghton, T. , Mather, J. Billinge, U. Pining, A. Jolley, J. W. Knowles, ' W. Warburton, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (deputy clerk), and C. H. Ward (master). SATISFACTORY STATE OF PADCIATE SCHOOLS. The Superintendent reported that the schools were now free from infectioes disease, and that the health of the children was very good. SITUATIONS WANTED FOR BOYS. The CHAIRMAN said that the Schools Committee were wisl.tul to secure places for eligible boys who were in the school at Padgate. Mr. Mines had sent circulars to 24 tradesmen in the town.offering the services of the boys, but not a single application had been received. ' TOWNSHIPS REPORTED TO THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD. The CLERK said that the Union auditor had reported to the Local. Government Board, all those townships whose monthly statements had not been sent in within the prescribed time during the past year. ENTERTAINMENT AT THE W0(11E1101/3E. The CHAIRMAN announced that Mr. G. 'Thompson had arranged for an entertainment to take place at the Work- house the following evening. An invitation was given to all Gaardians and officals to attend, VOTE OF CONDOLENCE WITH THE CLERK. The CHAritiiths said that last meeting, he had to refer to the death of Mr. Ratcliffe, and he was sorry that now he had to refer to the death of the daughter of their Clerk (Mr. Sutton). He moved that a vote of condol- ence and sympathy be passed to him in his sad bereavement. Mr. Davies seconded the motion, and referred to Miss Sutton's active interest in the affairs of Frodsham and the neighbourhood. The motion was carried unanimously and Mr. Sutton, in reply, said that lie thanked the Board for their gener- ous sympathy. His loss was one he could not express. APPLICATION FOR THE POST OF HAIRDRESSER. The Cuarems.e read a letter which had been received from Mr. James Stirrup, Buttermarket-street, offering his services as hairdresser to the Union for the ensuing year. He offered to discharge the duties of the office to the entire satisfaction of the Board. He stated that he had been a ratepayer for the past 11 years, and he sincerely trusted that the Board would see its way to give him the post. The CLERK said that the post was formerly advertised for, but at the present time it was held by a man ap- pointed by salary. Until the post was vacant, the letter could not be entertained. The post had not been for tender during the past six years.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON. Week ending 23rd February 1895. 1 During the week, which was the Bth of the current year, the following births and deaths were f, registered Births. Deattus. Warrington 35 27 . Latchford 6 2 , Total 4l 29 The excess of births over deaths was 12. The death. rate from all causes was equal to 264 per 1,000, against. 193 the average of the previous four weeks, and against • 14.5 the corresponding week of last year. The death' rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 21 per:1,000. against 15 the average rate of the four . previous weeks, and against 2 8 the corresponding week ot 1894. The ages of mortality were- -0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65 up. 5 7 2 1 9 3 27 —l— 1 2 . 1 • The deaths included-2 from scarlet fevor, 1 from whooping cough, 3 from phthisis, 1 from cancer, 2 from old age, 5 from brain disease, 6 from lung disease, 1 from drowning. The following cases of infectious disease have been . reported during the week :—Scarlet fever, 13 cages enteric fever, 1 case; puerperal fever, 2 cases. Total number of patients in hospital, week ending 23rd Feb., 1 32. • Meteorology.—The mean temperature was 30 Fah., I and therefore 4degs. higher than the preceding Week' The maximum temperature 41 F.. was recorded on the 22nd inst., and the minimum temperature 18 F., on the 20th inst. The rainfall measured nil inch. The general direction was N.E., the readink of the anemometer denoting 695 miles. - JOHN H. GORNALL, , Medical Officer of Health.
